# Nkambe Bakery & Recipe Handbook

Bread • pastry • savoury food • frozen desserts • juice & yogurt preservation

Integrated working edition | 3 October 2026

Prepared from the accessible documents and bakery discussions dated or modified 4 September–3 October 2026.

Practical recipes, consistent methods and a measured route from pilot batch to daily production.

This is a development and operating handbook. Formulations, yields, equipment capabilities and commercial assumptions require the trials identified in each section. A recipe being fully written does not mean it has been physically tested or approved for sale.

The source and editorial record accompanies this handbook. It identifies the 27 discovered files, duplicate publications, retained variants, corrections, evidence limits and additional preservation references.

<!--TOC-->

# 1. How to use this handbook

Begin with the shared controls, select a small opening menu, and print the relevant recipe and component cards for the station. Use one approved version per product. Enter actual batch results before increasing volume.

| Identifier | Meaning | How to use it |
|---|---|---|
| O-B01–O-B36 | October shared bakery and savoury components | Make these before assembling the O-P and O-A products. |
| O-P01–O-P20; O-A01–O-A04 | October finished products and four additions | Follow their component weights and service instructions. |
| O-FBASE1–7; O-F01–8 | October frozen bases and sale formats | Confirm machine suitability, treatment and cold chain first. |
| S-B/P/F/D/I/Y | September specialist recipes | Separate formulas, retained for their different product styles. S-F means fast food, whereas O-F means frozen. |
| T01 | Your cake-donut cracking trial | A controlled test based on your actual formula. |
| V series | Earlier recipes and development variants | Preserve ideas and formula differences; resolve the stated gaps before release. |

All principal formula quantities are grams, including milk, oil, eggs without shells, drained fish and prepared vegetables. The V-series preserves original litres, counts and ambiguous ingredients explicitly; these need weighing and specification before conversion. Only water may be approximated as 1 g/ml. A nominal egg count does not replace edible egg mass.

Baker’s percentages use flour as 100%; other percentages use the stated component basis. Do not mix them. Scale every ingredient by the same factor, then verify equipment load, mixing, heat penetration and cooling afresh. Larger batches are not validated by arithmetic alone.

Raw piece mass, cooked component mass and cooled sale weight are different measurements. Modelled loss is an assumption. Whole pieces = floor(usable batch mass ÷ actual portion mass). Record leftovers, rejects and samples. Air in ice cream increases volume, not ingredient mass.

October cost examples remain illustrative FCFA assumptions. September workbook prices and operating costs are largely unfilled; a missing price is unknown, never zero. The recipe IDs retain their original suffix so the source workbook remains traceable.

Use customer feedback to choose sweetness, size and price. Keep accessible descriptions alongside familiar product names. Clearly identify wheat, egg, milk, fish, groundnut, sesame and other supplier-declared allergens; vegetarian, dairy-free and allergen-free claims require ingredient and cross-contact checks.

Completeness is relative to the sources found: 27 files across the project, bakery chat-linked working folders and relevant Downloads, plus the cake-donut discussion. Google Drive and Pages searches produced no additional in-window bakery documents. Creation dates were not always available; modification dates and documented conversation context were used. No access to every private folder or external account is claimed.

# 2. Shared preparation, cooking and storage controls

## H01 — Hygiene, meat, eggs and controlled cooling

Use approved suppliers, potable water, cleanable surfaces and separate raw/cooked tools. Reject damaged tins, spoiled ingredients and unknown cold-chain lots. Store raw meat below and apart from ready-to-eat ingredients. Allocate different utensils and personnel tasks for raw poultry, cooked foods and bakery packing. Check flour, milk, eggs, fish, groundnut, sesame, mustard and sauce allergens; “may contain” statements do not replace cleaning or accurate supplier records [S34].

Use the common house cooking/reheating target of 75°C for 30 seconds for the meat, fish, poultry and cooked egg dishes in this handbook. Hold hot perishables at 63°C or above. These are operating targets informed by external guidance, not statements of Cameroonian law [N08]. Probe the coldest representative center with a calibrated sanitized thermometer; record time, temperature and corrective action. For this edition’s internal service rule, hold hot perishables at 63°C or above and chilled ones at 4°C or below. These conservative operating targets are not presented as Cameroonian statutory limits.

For cooked perishables to be stored, use active shallow-pan cooling. FDA’s reference benchmark is 57°C to 21°C within 2 hours and to 5°C or below within 6 hours total [S23, sections 3-501.14/.15]. The bakery’s stricter final storage target is 4°C. Map the thickest, largest and slowest-cooling portions; an ordinary fridge filled with hot deep pots is not a validated cooler. If a limit fails or history is missing, quarantine and withhold sale; do not improvise repeated reheating as a rescue. Qualified local review must establish disposition.

At trial stage, sell hot assembled foods promptly or use a monitored hot/chilled process. Log cumulative time outside refrigeration during portioning and proofing; schedule immediate cooking. Use an internal maximum 2-hour ambient preparation allowance, reduced to 1 hour in rooms above 32°C, as a conservative action threshold—not a permission to leave every food out that long. Any product-specific formal time-as-control scheme needs its own approval and written procedure. If the filling cannot remain safe during proofing, reduce batches or defer it.

## H02 — Dairy mix treatment and freezing

Use homogenized pasteurized/UHT milk and cream with certificates. Mixing powder into pasteurized milk can reintroduce contamination, so ingredient status alone does not validate the final mix. Obtain the actual STAB 2000 technical sheet and label. The proposed low-fat mixes use 0.40–0.45%, while the richer mix uses approximately 0.27%, within the supplier’s differentiated range [S14]. A changed stabilizer requires a new formulation; do not substitute starch, xanthan or an unnamed premix at the same dose.

Premix stabilizer into sugar before dispersing, allow supplier-directed hydration, and use a qualified dairy treatment system. Guelph’s batch reference is at least 69°C for 30 minutes throughout the mix [S09]. This edition does not translate continuous HTST seconds into an unvalidated pot process. A trained process specialist must establish the cold point, agitation, thermometer accuracy, actual hold, lid/condensate management and sanitation. Additional heating needed for supplier hydration must be specified in that approved program. If the pot cannot meet it, use a qualified pasteurizer or approved ready mix; do not sell experimental pot-treated dairy.

Cool promptly to 4°C or below using a mapped cooling method; do not leave a 10 kg mix on the bench. Age at 2–4°C only within the approved schedule. Treat post-process additions as ready-to-eat hazards. A stick blender is a dispersion tool, not a 50–200 bar homogenizer; trials must determine stability with the specified pre-homogenized dairy [S10].

Air increases ice-cream volume, not edible ingredient mass. Overrun = (mass of equal volume liquid mix ÷ mass of equal volume ice cream − 1) × 100. For a measured density of 1.08 kg/L and 30% overrun, 1 kg mix corresponds to about 1.20 L product before transfer losses; this is an illustrative calculation, not a machine result [S12]. Use actual density and mass portions for costing. Hardening and storage are separate capacities [S13]; do not put warm production into an overloaded holding freezer.

## H03 — Fruit ice and infusion controls

Acidity, ginger, sugar and freezing do not sterilize fruit. Use ready-to-eat treated inputs or a qualified product-specific process. FDA’s juice framework emphasizes pathogen reduction, not a universal kettle temperature for every fruit [S24]. Record pH, °Brix, fruit solids, maximum pulp size, treatment, cooling and sanitary filling. Supplier hydration guidance for Super Neutrose is not itself a pathogen-kill validation [S15]. If the treatment specification is missing, the formula is a laboratory/pilot development card only and cannot be commercially released.

Use a cleaned refractometer calibrated with water and temperature compensation. °Brix is a sucrose-equivalent refractive reading: acids and other soluble materials affect it. It is not an exact total-sugar assay. Hold fruit-source composition constant during the first trial. The old habit of writing “18–22°Brix” without calculating syrup/juice solids has been removed.

## H04 — Yogurt fermentation and cooling

Yogurt uses defined lactic cultures, controlled heat treatment, incubation and refrigeration. Tetra Pak describes a 90–95°C, 5-minute milk treatment and pH-triggered cooling; the pot must be qualified for the real fortified milk [S18]. The trial starter is Yogourmet Original, provisionally 3 g per litre milk in the source formulas, containing S. thermophilus, L. bulgaricus and L. acidophilus [S19, S20]. The current manufacturer page specifies one sachet per litre and 42–44°C; verify the mass on the actual regional sachet before using the 6 g recipe entries. The manufacturer’s domestic consumption window is not transferred to commercial fortified or fruit yogurt. This is an accessible named trial choice, not necessarily the least-cost commercial culture. Obtain supplier confirmation for added sugar/milk solids and planned production; switch to a commercial DVS culture only with its own dosing units and instructions.

Calibrate the pH meter with pH 4.00 and 7.00 buffers at the appropriate temperature each day. Record milk lot, culture lot, dose, inoculation temperature, readings during acidification and cooling. Start cooling at pH 4.5 as the trial endpoint; this alone does not validate safety. Establish an acceptable time-to-pH curve over repeated trials. If a batch misses the validated curve, shows gas or odor, or fails to acidify, quarantine it; do not taste or extend incubation indefinitely.

For small-cup trial engineering, aim to cool from incubation temperature to 20°C or below within 1 hour and to 4°C or below within 2 hours after endpoint. These are proposed stricter internal cooling targets to verify, not an industrial guarantee from a domestic refrigerator. Avoid disturbing set yogurt during gel formation. Stirred and drinking products need different controlled shear. No ambient shelf-stable yogurt is authorized by these recipes.

## H05 — Formula, scaling and shelf-life conventions

All ingredient amounts are grams; eggs exclude shells and prepared vegetables exclude trim. Water is potable. Instant Red/Gold mean the specified instant dry type, not fresh or active dry yeast. A manufacturer-authorized conversion and a fresh trial are required when yeast type changes; no gram-for-gram interchange is allowed. Flour strength and absorption must be checked by supplier data and actual development, not by a T-number alone.

Each component table states pilot mass, baker’s percent if flour is present (flour = 100), otherwise percent of that component, and a production multiplier. Dough hydration estimates include milk/egg/butter water, using declared development assumptions. For multicomponent products, filling percentages are component percentages, not dough baker’s percentages. Frozen and yogurt cards also report whole-product mix analysis where meaningful.

Mass reconciliation is explicit: component input minus handling/unretained allowance minus process loss equals retained output. Process loss applies to post-handling material. Divide by stated count; scale ingredients, losses and count by the same multiplier. Fried oil absorption and shell coatings are separate positive ingredient components. Oil-bath charge/replacement, pan grease, release aids and packaging are not silently included in edible net weight: record and cost them as process consumables/packaging. No remainder is hidden; theoretical component outputs are allocated equally across the count. Round individual weights only after choosing a production specification, and record any remainder or short count.

Loss percentages are planning hypotheses, not measurements. For cooking with fat drain and vapor, the initial combined process-loss estimate must be separated in trials by weighing drains and pre/post-cook batches. Evaporation during covered dairy treatment is initially modeled as zero; weigh before and after and update if material. Do not add untreated water after pasteurization to recover a target mass. Packaging mass is separate from edible net weight.

Every card requires first-off inspection plus at least ten representative raw/finished weights when batch count permits (all pieces in smaller pilots). Run three repeat batches before approving a working formula. Check retained plain bread/sweet products at 0, 6, 24 and 48 hours for quality; biscuits at days 0, 3, 7 and 14 with water-activity/packaging and microbiology review; refrigerated dairy at days 0, 1, 3 and 7 under a laboratory-agreed protocol; frozen products at days 0, 7, 14 and 28 with temperature records. These are study points, NOT approved sale-by periods. Spoiled or microbiologically suspect samples must never be tasted. Shelf-life and any safety-related labeling require product-specific assessment.

## Commercial release and preservation

The study days in H05 are sampling times, not shelf lives. Neither a same-day policy nor a seven-day trial window makes an unvalidated process safe. Until the product-specific treatment, sanitation, storage and release procedure is established, use development batches for controlled evaluation only. Approved ready-to-use treated ingredients may simplify commissioning.

Do not use the dairy pot for raw meat. Use dedicated raw-food containers and tools, or a documented sanitation changeover validated for the equipment. A scraper is not automatically a high-shear blender, aerator, dough mixer or pasteurizer. Follow the equipment’s actual working capacity and operating instructions.

Cooling benchmarks apply to cooked perishables; yogurt has its own endpoint and cooling plan. When this handbook refers to H01–H05 or F0, apply H01–H05 here and the frozen-control section. The common cold storage target is 4°C or below, with dairy aging at 2–4°C and frozen stock at −18°C or colder. The FDA reference cooling endpoint of 5°C is retained as a benchmark, followed by the house 4°C storage target.

# 3. Equipment, capacity and the opening menu

| Item | Owner-confirmed information | Still needed |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Rotary oven | 16 trays; 40 × 60 cm; diesel heating; reported 3 kW electrical | Model, diesel litres/hour, maximum temperature, rack load, clearances and actual bake uniformity |
| Production power | Reported 20 kW inverter; 30 kWh battery storage | Continuous AC rating, phase distribution, surge rating, usable battery fraction, discharge current and PV panel kWp |
| Sales power | Reported 15 kW inverter; 30 kWh battery storage | Same details; input charging limits are not necessarily output limits |
| Mixing/forming | Spiral 6.5 kW; moulder 1 kW; mixer 2 kW; professional mixing pot | Minimum/maximum dough mass; pot heating/agitation specification |
| Heat appliances | Proofers 1 kW; fryers 5 kW; cookers 5 kW; shawarma 3.5 kW | Number of units per rating, electrical phase, fryer litres and recovery |
| Ice cream | Two machines: 4 kW and 1.5 kW; described as soft serve and gravity | Model and feed method of each; approved mixes, hopper cooling, minimum charge and cleaning cycle |
| Popsicles | Two mould sizes; one is twice the other | Working fill volumes, cavities, machine power, cycle time and brine specification |
| Cold chain | Owner reports substantial refrigeration/freezing at both sites | Measured pull-down capacity, temperature logs, space, connected load and backup runtime |
| Other | Bread slicer available | Slicer power, blender/hot plate availability, scales, probes, insulated transport equipment |

“Gravity” and “soft serve” are not mutually exclusive machine categories. A manufacturer’s catalogue explicitly lists gravity-fed soft-serve machines. The plan uses one conditional soft-serve trial base; it does not infer that the second machine is a batch freezer. Obtain model plates before choosing settings or loading homemade mix. [Carpigiani soft serve categories](https://www.carpigiani.com/us/product-category/soft-serve-machines)

The new 30 kWh battery figure is recorded as nominal energy at each site. Solar array capacity remains unknown. The oven’s diesel supplies the heat: count both its electrical auxiliaries and diesel consumption. The electrical rating alone is not its full energy cost.

## Batch and tray capacity

A 40 × 60 cm tray has 0.24 m² of surface; sixteen trays have 3.84 m² before spacing and usable-area losses. That does not specify how many tins fit, the permissible rack weight, dough-mixer capacity or hourly output. Draw a tray layout with the actual tins. A trial of 12 milk buns per tray would mean 192 buns per rack, but it is an illustration, not a rated capacity. Do not fill all sixteen trays until airflow and bake uniformity have been checked.

The recipe library gives pilot quantities and a x5 planning production quantity. No mixer bowl or minimum machine charge has been verified. Use a smaller mixer or hand pilot if the spiral cannot mix the pilot load safely. Production quantities are for purchasing and scale-up trials, not permission to overload equipment. The professional mixing pot is not assumed to be a validated pasteurizer.

## Weights and formula conventions

All formulation quantities are grams, including liquids, to avoid density errors. Water alone may be approximated as 1 g/ml. Milk, oil, condensed milk and fruit puree must be weighed. An egg is provisionally 50 g edible contents; use the weighed mass, not a fixed count. Vegetable prices are per peeled/trimmed edible kilogram; meat is boneless purchase weight; canned fish is drained weight. Convert supplier gross-pack prices before use.

Yields are trial estimates after stated losses. Cost cooked components by saleable output. Frying oil entries cover usage/disposal, not the initial fryer fill; budget the initial fill separately as working stock.

Build the opening business around six dependable core products: family bread, condensed milk buns, sugar doughnuts, puff puff, fish rolls and beef meat pies. Add chicken shawarma only when its service station and raw-meat workflow pass a live trial. Start the frozen counter with one approved soft-serve base and small milk pops; add the larger pops only after customers accept the price and freezer throughput is measured.

This is a commercial hypothesis, not a forecast of Nkambe demand. No current Nkambe customer survey, supplier quotations, competitor-weight audit, footfall count or sales history was supplied. The menu prices below are test points, not observed local market prices. Product margins use the same explicit illustrative ingredient-price list throughout.

There is a documented local sweet potato processing and bakery initiative. CLGF reported the Nkambe project in September 2025; that supports exploring a local supplier and product story, but does not establish today’s supply, consumer demand or the performance of this report’s 15% sweet potato formula. [CLGF project report](https://www.clgf.org.uk/whats-new/news/decentralisation-in-cameroon-yielding-results/)

| Rank | Opening priority | Decision condition |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | O-P01 Family bread | Blind comparison against locally purchased loaves at matched weight |
| 2 | O-P02 Milk buns | Target customers accept price after tasting |
| 3 | O-P04 Sugar doughnuts | Two consecutive batches meet oil, yield and texture checks |
| 4 | O-P06 Fish rolls | Chilled filling, cooking and transport logs pass |
| 5 | O-P07 Beef pies | Filling portion and cost remain controlled |
| 6 | O-P05 Puff puff | Sell in small waves; labour and oil do not erase contribution |
| 7 | O-P13 Chicken shawarma | Only after service drill and safe cooking verification |
| 8 | O-F01 Soft serve or O-F05 milk pop | Choose one first, based on manufacturer approval and cold-chain drill |

Make O-P03 sweet potato bread a small paid test, not the opening volume anchor. Keep pizza, beef shawarma, burgers, fried-chicken meals and dairy cake for later phases; they add value but also create different queues, allergens and temperature-control demands. The full 20-product library is a development range, not a recommended opening-day menu.

## Decisions carried forward from earlier documents

The older divider, planetary mixer, slicer and sheeter proposals remain conditional on measured bottlenecks. The latest inventory says a slicer is already available. Confirm its condition before purchasing another. A planetary mixer supports cake creaming and pastry work; a sheeter supports lamination; neither automatically has the best return. Request working loads, minimum loads, phase, voltage, rated and starting power, spare parts, manuals, warranty and a test with your actual product. Quote delivered cost and maintenance, not purchase price alone.

Older records describe the spiral as 120 L / 50 kg flour and 2.8/5.6 kW; the October plan gives 6.5 kW. Treat these as unresolved nameplate differences. Do not size wiring, inverter or batch mass from the conflicting claims. A VFD is not a universal solution for arbitrary three-phase machinery; have compatibility checked for the exact motor and supply.

At five baguettes per tray, a 16-tray rack holds 80, not 101. The earlier 18 kg flour, 11.88 kg combined water/ice, 360 g salt and 90 g yeast batch totals 30.33 kg: 101 × 300 g before losses, requiring more than one 80-piece rack. A 300 g piece with 15% bake loss weighs 255 g, not 250 g. Starting mixes every 45 minutes cannot continuously fill an oven cycling every 25 minutes; measure the whole line and schedule around its slowest stage.

Keep ice inside the total formula water. Choose chilled water and ice from measured dough temperature and friction, not a fixed ice percentage. Protect hot bread from road dust using clean ventilated food-grade transport; cool before sealing. Do not apply raw egg after baking. Direct combustion exhaust outdoors through a compliant installation, away from food and customers.

Offer sliced bread, household loaves and breakfast bundles as paid tests. Agege and tangzhong breads remain development opportunities. A soft crumb is a quality attribute, not proof of microbial shelf life. Avoid promises of free solar energy, guaranteed premium prices, instant market dominance or automatic profit from residual oven heat.

# 4. Shared bakery and savoury components

Sweetened condensed milk supplies added sugar, milk solids and water. It is different from unsweetened evaporated milk and dry milk powder. For development calculations only, assume 45% added sucrose, 27% water and 28% milk solids including about 8% fat; these fractions sum to 100%. Naturally occurring lactose is inside the milk-solids fraction, so do not add total label sugars a second time.

Check the purchased label. One manufacturer example reports 55.9 g total sugars and 8.1 g fat per 100 g; total sugars are not the same as added sucrose. No brand specification is assumed to apply to your local tin. [Nestlé example product specification](https://www.nestleprofessional.com.au/nestle-milks/sweetened-condensed-milk-395g)

In O-B02, 150 g condensed milk adds approximately 67.5 g sucrose, 40.5 g water and 42 g milk solids, in addition to the separately listed 80 g sugar and 430 g water. The trial substitution on O-P02 approximates those functions, not identical flavour. For cost: 150 g condensed milk at 3,000 FCFA/kg costs 450 FCFA; 68 g sugar + 42 g whole milk powder + 40 g water costs about 244 FCFA, saving roughly 206 FCFA per O-B02 pilot, before any quality/yield change. Test both blind.

Use condensed milk selectively in buns, banana cake, soaked cake and frozen dairy products. Keep O-P01 bread, plain puff puff and most savouries free of it in the baseline to protect entry-level cost. Added dairy glaze changes the holding requirements even if the underlying bread is normally sold at ambient temperature.

Use these component formulas with their linked finished-product cards. Costs are illustrative ingredient costs only. Each card’s production column is a planning scale, not an approved machine load. Portions and additional egg wash, coatings, frying oil and packaging appear on the finished-product cards.

## O-B01 Family bread dough

Pilot input 1,715 g; target usable output 1,700 g. Planning production batch is x5: input 8,575 g and usable output 8,500 g. These are trial targets; machine minimum/maximum batch capacities remain unverified.

| Ingredient or prepared component | Pilot g | Production g | Baker % |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Bread flour | 1,000 | 5,000 | 100.00% |
| Water | 600 | 3,000 | 60.00% |
| Sugar | 50 | 250 | 5.00% |
| Margarine | 35 | 175 | 3.50% |
| Salt | 18 | 90 | 1.80% |
| Yeast | 12 | 60 | 1.20% |

Baker’s percentages use 1,000 g total flour as 100%; added milk/egg/fat are weighed ingredients, not equivalent to water.

1. Weigh ingredients. Reserve 30 g of the listed water; add only if the flour absorbs it. The stated yield assumes all 600 g is used.

2. Mix flour, water, sugar and yeast about 3 minutes at low speed. Add salt, then mix 4–7 minutes at the mixer’s dough-development speed. Add margarine during the final 2 minutes. Stop at a thin elastic window and a dough temperature near 26–28°C.

3. Rest covered 15 minutes. Divide, pre-round, rest 10 minutes, then mould and tin as specified on the product card.

4. Trial proof at 30–35°C with enough humidity to prevent skinning, approximately 45–75 minutes. Bake when a gentle indentation returns slowly; do not use the clock alone.

Yield and process note: Input 1,715 g; allow 15 g bowl/handling loss. No improver assumed. If one is used, follow its label and re-cost.

Illustrative ingredient cost: 835 FCFA per pilot batch; 491.3 FCFA/kg usable output. This excludes packaging and operating costs.

## O-B02 Condensed milk bun dough

Pilot input 1,895 g; target usable output 1,870 g. Planning production batch is x5: input 9,475 g and usable output 9,350 g. These are trial targets; machine minimum/maximum batch capacities remain unverified.

| Ingredient or prepared component | Pilot g | Production g | Baker % |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Bread flour | 1,000 | 5,000 | 100.00% |
| Water | 430 | 2,150 | 43.00% |
| Condensed milk | 150 | 750 | 15.00% |
| Sugar | 80 | 400 | 8.00% |
| Egg | 100 | 500 | 10.00% |
| Margarine | 80 | 400 | 8.00% |
| Whole milk powder | 20 | 100 | 2.00% |
| Salt | 16 | 80 | 1.60% |
| Yeast | 15 | 75 | 1.50% |
| Vanilla | 4 | 20 | 0.40% |

Baker’s percentages use 1,000 g total flour as 100%; added milk/egg/fat are weighed ingredients, not equivalent to water.

1. Dissolve condensed milk in water. Mix with flour, sugar, milk powder, yeast and egg on low for 3 minutes; reserve 25 g water until dough consistency is clear.

2. Add salt, develop 4–6 minutes, then add margarine gradually and mix another 2–4 minutes to an elastic film. Target 26–28°C dough; stop before overheating.

3. Rest covered 15 minutes. Portion and round with a tight surface; use the product card for bun weights.

4. Proof around 30–35°C for approximately 50–80 minutes until light and a gentle indentation returns slowly.

Yield and process note: Input 1,895 g; 25 g handling allowance. Egg wash is listed separately on the product card.

Illustrative ingredient cost: 1,765 FCFA per pilot batch; 943.6 FCFA/kg usable output. This excludes packaging and operating costs.

## O-B03 Sweet potato milk bread dough

Pilot input 1,812 g; target usable output 1,790 g. Planning production batch is x5: input 9,060 g and usable output 8,950 g. These are trial targets; machine minimum/maximum batch capacities remain unverified.

| Ingredient or prepared component | Pilot g | Production g | Baker % |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Bread flour | 850 | 4,250 | 85.00% |
| Sweet potato flour | 150 | 750 | 15.00% |
| Water | 570 | 2,850 | 57.00% |
| Condensed milk | 100 | 500 | 10.00% |
| Sugar | 40 | 200 | 4.00% |
| Margarine | 50 | 250 | 5.00% |
| Whole milk powder | 20 | 100 | 2.00% |
| Salt | 18 | 90 | 1.80% |
| Yeast | 14 | 70 | 1.40% |

Baker’s percentages use 1,000 g total flour as 100%; added milk/egg/fat are weighed ingredients, not equivalent to water.

1. Blend the two flours thoroughly; baker’s percentages use their combined 1,000 g. Reserve 40 g water for absorption adjustment.

2. Mix all except margarine for 3 minutes slowly, then develop 5–8 minutes. Add margarine and mix until extensible. Expect a less strong window than wheat-only bread; excessive mixing will not restore absent gluten.

3. Rest 15 minutes, divide and mould gently. Proof around 30–33°C to a resilient loaf, stopping before the surface weakens.

4. Use the product card for baking. Record actual loaf volume, sweetness and day-of-sale softness before increasing sweet potato flour.

Yield and process note: Input 1,812 g; 22 g handling allowance. Start at 15% sweet potato flour, not an untested high replacement ratio.

Illustrative ingredient cost: 1,309 FCFA per pilot batch; 731.0 FCFA/kg usable output. This excludes packaging and operating costs.

## O-B04 Yeast doughnut dough

Pilot input 1,808 g; target usable output 1,780 g. Planning production batch is x5: input 9,040 g and usable output 8,900 g. These are trial targets; machine minimum/maximum batch capacities remain unverified.

| Ingredient or prepared component | Pilot g | Production g | Baker % |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Bread flour | 1,000 | 5,000 | 100.00% |
| Water | 440 | 2,200 | 44.00% |
| Egg | 100 | 500 | 10.00% |
| Sugar | 120 | 600 | 12.00% |
| Margarine | 80 | 400 | 8.00% |
| Whole milk powder | 30 | 150 | 3.00% |
| Salt | 15 | 75 | 1.50% |
| Yeast | 18 | 90 | 1.80% |
| Nutmeg | 2 | 10 | 0.20% |
| Vanilla | 3 | 15 | 0.30% |

Baker’s percentages use 1,000 g total flour as 100%; added milk/egg/fat are weighed ingredients, not equivalent to water.

1. Mix flour, water, egg, sugar, milk powder and yeast 3 minutes slowly. Add salt and mix 4–6 minutes; finish by incorporating margarine, nutmeg and vanilla.

2. Stop at elastic dough near 26–28°C. Rest covered 15 minutes; weigh individual pieces instead of repeatedly rerolling scraps.

3. Round or form rings; proof around 30°C for approximately 40–65 minutes until light, not fragile. Uncover only briefly to make transfer manageable.

4. Fry as specified on the product card; use a thermometer and allow oil temperature to recover between loads.

Yield and process note: Input 1,808 g; 28 g handling allowance. No filling is included.

Illustrative ingredient cost: 1,417 FCFA per pilot batch; 796.2 FCFA/kg usable output. This excludes packaging and operating costs.

## O-B05 Puff puff batter

Pilot input 1,846 g; target usable output 1,830 g. Planning production batch is x5: input 9,230 g and usable output 9,150 g. These are trial targets; machine minimum/maximum batch capacities remain unverified.

| Ingredient or prepared component | Pilot g | Production g | Baker % |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Plain flour | 1,000 | 5,000 | 100.00% |
| Water | 700 | 3,500 | 70.00% |
| Sugar | 120 | 600 | 12.00% |
| Salt | 12 | 60 | 1.20% |
| Yeast | 12 | 60 | 1.20% |
| Nutmeg | 2 | 10 | 0.20% |

Baker’s percentages use 1,000 g total flour as 100%; added milk/egg/fat are weighed ingredients, not equivalent to water.

1. Mix dry ingredients, then add water and beat 4–6 minutes to a cohesive sticky batter without dry flour.

2. Cover and ferment near 26–30°C for approximately 45–75 minutes, until bubbly and substantially expanded. Stir once gently if bubbles are very uneven.

3. Use a calibrated scoop to deliver 30 g portions. Trial fry one piece before loading the fryer.

4. Fry at 165–175°C, turning for even colour and checking the centre is set, typically 4–6 minutes depending on size.

Yield and process note: Input 1,846 g; 16 g bowl loss. Fermentation time is conditional on temperature.

Illustrative ingredient cost: 846 FCFA per pilot batch; 462.5 FCFA/kg usable output. This excludes packaging and operating costs.

## O-B06 Fish roll dough

Pilot input 1,679 g; target usable output 1,660 g. Planning production batch is x5: input 8,395 g and usable output 8,300 g. These are trial targets; machine minimum/maximum batch capacities remain unverified.

| Ingredient or prepared component | Pilot g | Production g | Baker % |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Plain flour | 1,000 | 5,000 | 100.00% |
| Water | 470 | 2,350 | 47.00% |
| Margarine | 100 | 500 | 10.00% |
| Sugar | 60 | 300 | 6.00% |
| Whole milk powder | 20 | 100 | 2.00% |
| Salt | 15 | 75 | 1.50% |
| Yeast | 12 | 60 | 1.20% |
| Nutmeg | 2 | 10 | 0.20% |

Baker’s percentages use 1,000 g total flour as 100%; added milk/egg/fat are weighed ingredients, not equivalent to water.

1. Mix flour, sugar, powder, yeast and water on low for 3 minutes. Add salt, margarine and nutmeg; mix another 4–6 minutes until smooth, with moderate gluten development.

2. Rest covered 20 minutes. Divide and roll as specified, without making the wrapper excessively thick.

3. Use fully chilled filling; seal clean dough edges firmly, with no filling trapped in the seam.

4. Rest assembled rolls 15–25 minutes before frying. They should be slightly aerated rather than fully doubled.

Yield and process note: Input 1,679 g; 19 g handling loss.

Illustrative ingredient cost: 1,068 FCFA per pilot batch; 643.6 FCFA/kg usable output. This excludes packaging and operating costs.

## O-B07 Shortcrust pie pastry

Pilot input 1,773 g; target usable output 1,740 g. Planning production batch is x5: input 8,865 g and usable output 8,700 g. These are trial targets; machine minimum/maximum batch capacities remain unverified.

| Ingredient or prepared component | Pilot g | Production g | Baker % |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Plain flour | 1,000 | 5,000 | 100.00% |
| Margarine | 400 | 2,000 | 40.00% |
| Water | 290 | 1,450 | 29.00% |
| Egg | 60 | 300 | 6.00% |
| Salt | 15 | 75 | 1.50% |
| Baking powder | 8 | 40 | 0.80% |

Baker’s percentages use 1,000 g total flour as 100%; added milk/egg/fat are weighed ingredients, not equivalent to water.

1. Keep fat and water cool. Mix flour, salt and baking powder; rub in margarine to coarse crumbs.

2. Add egg and most of the water. Fold just until cohesive; add the remainder only if needed. Do not develop a bread-like gluten network.

3. Cover and refrigerate 30–45 minutes. Roll to about 3 mm on a lightly floured surface.

4. Weigh cut portions, use chilled filling and seal. Rework clean trimmings only once; include any additional scrap in the recorded yield loss.

Yield and process note: Input 1,773 g; 33 g planned trimming/bowl loss after limited rework. Dusting flour must be recorded if material.

Illustrative ingredient cost: 1,541 FCFA per pilot batch; 885.9 FCFA/kg usable output. This excludes packaging and operating costs.

## O-B13 Hard boiled peeled eggs

Pilot input 1,000 g; target usable output 970 g. Planning production batch is x5: input 5,000 g and usable output 4,850 g. These are trial targets; machine minimum/maximum batch capacities remain unverified.

| Ingredient or prepared component | Pilot g | Production g | Baker % |

| Egg | 1,000 | 5,000 | — |

1. The formula uses edible egg weight. Purchase intact eggs, keep shell-handling separate, and wash hands after handling.

2. Lower into boiling water, simmer until yolks and whites are fully set; a starting trial is 10–12 minutes for medium eggs, then verify by cutting a sample.

3. Cool rapidly in potable ice water, peel hygienically and refrigerate. Weigh peeled cooked yield.

4. Portion by actual egg size; the model uses 50 g peeled eggs. Do not store peeled eggs at ambient temperature.

Yield and process note: 970 g expected cooked edible yield from 1,000 g raw edible equivalent. Shell cost is already converted into the egg price input.

Illustrative ingredient cost: 2,200 FCFA per pilot batch; 2,268.0 FCFA/kg usable output. This excludes packaging and operating costs.

## O-B15 Pizza dough

Pilot input 1,708 g; target usable output 1,690 g. Planning production batch is x5: input 8,540 g and usable output 8,450 g. These are trial targets; machine minimum/maximum batch capacities remain unverified.

Baker’s percentages use 1,000 g total flour as 100%; added milk/egg/fat are weighed ingredients, not equivalent to water.

1. Mix flour, water, sugar and yeast 3 minutes slowly; add salt and develop 5–7 minutes. Add oil near the end.

2. Cover and ferment approximately 60–90 minutes at 24–27°C until aerated. Fold once if needed.

3. Divide into 150 g balls, rest covered 20–30 minutes, then stretch to 18–20 cm without flattening every bubble at the rim.

4. Top immediately before baking; use cooked chicken and chilled sauce, not raw poultry.

Yield and process note: Input 1,708 g; 18 g handling loss.

2. Mix seasoning briefly, portion ten raw patties of approximately 102 g and make a shallow centre depression.

3. Cook on a lightly oiled hot plate, turning as needed, until the centre reaches the H01–H05 ground-beef limit. Probe from the side into the thickest centre.

4. Target ten 75 g cooked patties; weigh actual yield. Assemble immediately or hold under H01–H05.

| Yeast | 8 | 40 | 0.80% |
| Oil | 30 | 150 | 3.00% |

Baker’s percentages use 1,000 g total flour as 100%; added milk/egg/fat are weighed ingredients, not equivalent to water.

1. Mix all ingredients 3 minutes slowly then 5–7 minutes until smooth and extensible.

2. Rest covered 30 minutes, divide into sixteen pieces of about 104 g, then rest another 15 minutes.

3. Roll each evenly to about 24–26 cm. Cook on a very hot clean plate or in a 240–260°C trial oven until set and just blistered, roughly 1–3 minutes; avoid crisping.

4. Stack briefly under a clean cover to retain flexibility, then cool without condensation. Target 1,500 g cooked bread; sixteen wraps at 90 g consume 1,440 g.

Yield and process note: Input 1,681 g; allow about 17 g handling and 164 g baking loss. Extra 60 g cooked bread is allocated to QC/weight variation in product costing.

Illustrative ingredient cost: 768 FCFA per pilot batch; 512.2 FCFA/kg usable output. This excludes packaging and operating costs.

## O-B18 Cooked chicken shawarma meat

Preparation-ahead option: chapter 6 gives the full oven, cooling, portioning and reheating method. Its final-yield adjustments apply when this base is cooked, chilled and reheated.

Pilot input 1,304 g; target usable output 750 g. Planning production batch is x5: input 6,520 g and usable output 3,750 g. These are trial targets; machine minimum/maximum batch capacities remain unverified.

| Cumin | 6 | 30 | — |
| Coriander | 6 | 30 | — |
| Black pepper | 3 | 15 | — |

| Chili trimmed | 5 | 25 | — |

1. Use trimmed boneless thigh; keep raw meat at or below 4°C. Mix marinade separately, coat meat, cover, label and marinate refrigerated 6–12 hours as a trial quality window.

2. Drain only excess free marinade. Weigh the raw load. Use the vertical machine only once its minimum cone size and safe operating instructions are established; otherwise cook small batches on a suitable hot plate.

3. Follow the dedicated shawarma service workflow in chapter 6. Shave only the cooked outer layer, then finish slices on the griddle and probe to H01–H05 poultry temperature.

4. Target 750 g saleable cooked meat per 1,000 g purchased boneless meat. All marinade cost is charged even though some is discarded. Never reuse raw marinade as sauce.

Yield and process note: Gross input 1,304 g including marinade; 750 g cooked saleable output is an assumed purchase-to-cooked yield of 75%, not 75% of the marinated total.

Illustrative ingredient cost: 4,743 FCFA per pilot batch; 6,323.9 FCFA/kg usable output. This excludes packaging and operating costs.

## O-B36 lemon-garlic oven fish

This is a new shared base for O-A02/O-A03. Use supplier-identified, food-grade, boneless tilapia or another firm white-fish fillet. The recipe is for trimmed edible fillet, not whole fish. Inspect carefully for pin bones before and after cooking. Keep fish utensils separate; fish is an additional allergen in the burger/wrap station.

| Ingredient | Pilot g | Production trial g (×5) |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Boneless trimmed white fish | 1,000 | 5,000 |
| Oil | 25 | 125 |
| Lemon juice | 25 | 125 |
| Garlic, finely crushed | 15 | 75 |
| Salt | 10 | 50 |
| Paprika | 4 | 20 |
| Black pepper | 2 | 10 |
| Total input | 1,081 | 5,405 |

1. Keep fillets chilled, drain excess thaw water and pat with disposable food-safe towel. Cut pieces of similar thickness, roughly 15–20 mm; keep smaller pieces in a separate trial group. Mix the listed seasonings and oil, coat fish, and refrigerate for a short trial of 15–20 minutes. Acid does not make raw fish safe; extended lemon contact can soften it.

2. Place in a single layer in a lined raised-edge pan. Trial roast at 180–190°C, checking around 10–16 minutes; thickness and oven loading determine the actual duration. Meet H01–H05 at the thickest centre. Target moist flakes and light browning, with no raw centre. Inspect for remaining bones with clean utensils without breaking every piece unnecessarily.

3. For immediate service, portion after cooking and assemble. For preparation ahead, cool, portion and transport using the controlled route above. Trial reheating covered briefly to limit drying, then uncover if surface moisture needs removal; meet H01–H05 before assembly. Do not mix yesterday's fish with a new batch or use strongly deteriorated fish with extra spice.

4. Planning yield: 800 g chilled usable fish per pilot, then 760 g after a trial 95% reheating retention. These are unverified assumptions, not measured performance. Record cooking, chilling, trimming and reheating losses separately. At 120 g final fish, one pilot supports six sale portions and a 40 g final-equivalent balance; at 100 g, seven and a 60 g balance. Balances must have an identified same-day use or be charged as waste, not counted as full portions.

Illustrative raw fillet price: 4,500 FCFA/kg edible, newly assumed for costing only. Other inputs use chapter 12 prices. O-B36 pilot ingredients cost **4,684.0 FCFA**, or **6,163.2 FCFA/kg of final reheated output**. Whole-fish purchase cost per usable kg equals whole-fish price divided by measured fillet yield, before cooking losses. Supplier pricing and bone removal can materially change viability.

# 5. Bread, fried snacks and bakery meals

The finished cards use the shared components in chapter 4. Make the specified component amount, then assemble by weight. Alternatives in the S-series are separate product versions; do not blend their formulas accidentally.

## O-P01 Soft family bread

Quality target: Fine even crumb, thin golden crust, clean slices and a soft but fully baked centre.

Pilot: 3 saleable units. Planning production batch: 15 units. Portion: 3 x 550 g dough; about 470–495 g baked each. Scale all components x5; validate capacity and losses before adoption.

| Prepared component or ingredient | Pilot g | Production g |
| --- | --- | --- |
| O-B01 Family bread dough | 1,700 | 8,500 |

1. Make O-B01. Weigh three 550 g dough pieces; the 50 g surplus covers QC/test dough and is charged to these loaves.

2. Pre-round, rest 10 minutes and mould into greased tins. Proof as O-B01; leave room for oven spring rather than overproofing to the tin edge.

3. Trial bake at 195–205°C for 25–32 minutes. Check a representative loaf centre near 94–96°C as a baking-quality cue, not a universal safety limit.

4. De-tin immediately. Cool on racks until no warmth/condensation remains before slicing and bagging. Verify cooled sale weight and slicing consistency.

Holding and packaging: A — Unfilled bread/plain fried snack: quality policy is sale on production day after protected cooling. No multi-day ambient safety claim. Keep clean, dry and protected; record discard or separately approved reuse. Adding dairy glaze/filling changes the class.

Troubleshooting: Dense crumb: insufficient proof/development; collapsed top: excessive proof or underbake; gummy slices: sliced too warm.

Substitution: Use the strongest consistent local wheat flour; re-trial water rather than assuming equal absorption.

| Batch ingredient cost | Saleable units | Ingredients/unit | Pack/unit | Other variable/unit | Test price | Contribution/unit |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 835 | 3 | 278.4 | 30 | 55 | 650 | 286.6 |

All values in FCFA. Contribution margin 44.1%; this is before payroll, rent and other fixed overhead. 20% ingredient-price increase reduces contribution to 230.9 FCFA. At 80% sell-through it falls to 195.8 FCFA per sold unit under the chapter 12 model.

## O-P02 Condensed milk breakfast buns

Quality target: Rounded golden buns, shreddable soft crumb, clear milk aroma without a wet surface.

Pilot: 20 saleable units. Planning production batch: 100 units. Portion: 20 x 90 g dough; about 78–82 g baked. Scale all components x5; validate capacity and losses before adoption.

2. Round tightly, place with expansion space, proof using O-B02, then brush lightly with the 40 g weighed egg wash; discard unused raw-contact wash.

3. Bake at a trial 180–190°C for 14–18 minutes. Rotate only if safe and the oven’s distribution test indicates a need.

4. Cool completely and bag. Start with plain buns; an optional 8 g O-B34 glaze adds refrigerated handling and additional cost, so it is excluded from baseline pricing.

Holding and packaging: A — Unfilled bread/plain fried snack: quality policy is sale on production day after protected cooling. No multi-day ambient safety claim. Keep clean, dry and protected; record discard or separately approved reuse. Adding dairy glaze/filling changes the class.

Troubleshooting: Tight crumb: add fat too early or underproof; very dark crust: excessive sugar or heat; wrinkling: underbaking.

Substitution: Budget trial: replace each 150 g condensed milk with 68 g sugar, 42 g whole milk powder and 40 g water; sensory and cost recheck required.

## O-P06 Cameroon style fish rolls

Quality target: Crisp golden exterior, rolled seam and a clearly present dry fish filling.

2. Roll 65 g dough pieces, add 25 g filling, close edges and seam, then rest briefly as O-B06.

3. Fry at a trial 165–175°C until golden and filling is thoroughly reheated under H01–H05. Check the thickest sample.

4. Drain and use holding class H. Transport finished rolls hot under control; an alternative is chilled assembled uncooked rolls for final frying at the sales site under chapter 6. Do not chill finished rolls for a second reheating of the fish filling.

Holding and packaging: H — The final bake/fry reheats the previously cooked chilled filling or topping. Serve promptly or hold hot under H01–H05, with a house quality target of at most 2 hours. Do not cool finished leftovers for another reheat. Follow the product-specific transport and finishing route in chapter 6.

Troubleshooting: Burst seams: wet filling or poor seal; gummy spiral: wrapper too thick; fishy flavour: unsuitable fish/storage.

Substitution: Use cooked deboned fresh fish only with measured purchase-to-edible yield and revised cost.

## O-P08 Sausage rolls

Quality target: Crisp pastry with a fully cooked moist meat centre and clean slice.

Pilot: 24 saleable units. Planning production batch: 120 units. Portion: 24 x 65 g pastry + 40 g raw filling; about 88–96 g baked. Scale all components x5; validate capacity and losses before adoption.

| Prepared component or ingredient | Pilot g | Production g |
| --- | --- | --- |
| O-B07 Shortcrust pie pastry | 1,740 | 8,700 |
| O-B10 Sausage roll filling | 960 | 4,800 |
| Egg | 45 | 225 |

1. Make O-B07 and keep O-B10 cold. Portion 24 rolls with 65 g pastry and 40 g raw filling each. Pastry surplus is 180 g; O-B10 batch leaves 20 g.

2. Form cylinders, enclose and seal pastry, apply 45 g allocated egg wash and vent the tops.

3. Trial bake at 190–200°C for 23–30 minutes. Probe meat to H01–H05 ground-meat limit, or poultry limit if sausage contains poultry.

4. Hold and transport under H. Record any grease leakage before adjusting meat fat or crumb content.

Holding and packaging: H — Serve immediately or hold hot under H01–H05. With the specified raw sausage filling, a fully baked roll may instead be rapidly cooled and reheated once using chapter 6. House hot-display quality target: at most 2 hours. Record the actual filling heat history.

Troubleshooting: Raw centre: rolls too thick or oven too hot; greasy pastry: high-fat filling; splitting: poor vent/seal.

Substitution: Supplier-seasoned mince can replace sausage meat only after checking salt, species and allergen details.

Scale every other base by the pilot quantity divided by its listed usable yield. Warm the bread, reheat fish under H01–H05, cook fries fresh, then add weighed slaw, pickles and sauces. Fold gently so fish does not crush the bread; a short final toast is for the bread after the protein has met H01–H05. Serve immediately. The ten portions consume exactly the listed prepared component amounts; any whole-base leftovers require planned same-day use or a waste charge. Scale to 50 wraps by multiplying every pilot quantity by five. Allergens: fish, wheat/gluten and egg/mustard as present in sauces; check milk in purchased ingredients.

| --- | --- | --- |
| O-B15 pizza dough | 150 | 1,500 |
| O-B16 pizza sauce | 40 | 400 |
| O-B19 beef, final target after pizza bake | 50 | 500 |
| Mozzarella | 50 | 500 |
| Onion | 10 | 100 |
| Sweet pepper | 10 | 100 |

Use 526.3 g chilled O-B19 beef at the illustrative 95% final-bake retention: scale every O-B19 ingredient by 526.3/700 = 0.75188 (raw beef 751.9 g). Do not separately reheat the beef and then store it again. Prepare ten 150 g dough rounds using O-B15, add 40 g sauce, 52.6 g cold cooked beef, 50 g cheese and 10 g each onion/pepper. Trial bake using O-P12's oven method and check that the meat topping reaches H01–H05 during that same bake. The bake is the beef's one reheating step. Serve immediately or hold hot under H01–H05; do not cool unsold pizzas for another reheat. Target approximately 275–290 g finished pizza, verified by weighing; moisture losses in dough and vegetables are separate from the meat allowance. Scale to 50 pizzas by multiplying all ingredients by five. Allergens: wheat/gluten and milk, plus anything declared in purchased sauces.

## Vertical spit service workflow

1. Before service: verify raw storage, refrigerated garnish station, handwashing, cleaned probes, clean knives, ventilation and machine guards. Make a cone only as large as forecast sales and the manufacturer’s minimum allow; switch to small-batch pan cooking if those quantities conflict.

2. Keep raw marinated meat cold until loading. Cook continuously according to the machine instructions; do not turn off heat while leaving a partly cooked cone warm for later service. Never add raw meat on top of a cooking cone.

3. Shave thin slices only from a thoroughly cooked exterior using the designated knife. Finish sliced meat immediately on the hot plate; verify the H01–H05 house centre-temperature target. Exterior colour alone is insufficient.

4. Transfer cooked meat with clean utensils into a controlled hot holding pan or directly into a wrap. Never use the raw-meat tray or tongs for finished slices.

5. At close, use the conservative one-day cone policy: discard unsold cone and shaved meat. Do not refrigerate a partly cooked cone for the next day. Reduce tomorrow’s starting cone size if waste is excessive. [Local-authority doner safety guidance](https://www.brighton-hove.gov.uk/business-and-trade/food-safety/cook-high-risk-foods-safely/prepare-and-cook-doner-kebabs-safely)

6. Clean and sanitize raw station, knife, drip areas, hot plate and service surfaces according to equipment and chemical instructions; record disposal and close temperatures.

## Station and handover

Flow: refrigerated raw meat → dedicated cooker → clean finishing/hot-holding zone → wrap assembly → toast → packaging → customer. Beside assembly, keep slaw, pickles and sauces refrigerated with separate utensils. Put raw-meat prep physically away from ready-to-eat vegetables. One person handles money or washes hands and changes tasks before touching food. Use measured scoops verified on scales, not free pouring.

Service target after training: a standard wrap in 3–5 minutes when safe components are ready. During low demand, cook to order in smaller batches rather than maintaining an oversized cone. Optional 20 g cheese adds 130 FCFA ingredient cost at the assumed price before other costs; trial an add-on price of 250–300 FCFA. Do not add optional components without a portion and allergen instruction.

Yes: fully cook suitable meat at the production site, cool it rapidly, weigh and label chilled portions, transport them cold, and reheat only the portions ordered before assembly. This can suit the two-site business and avoids keeping a large shawarma cone running during quiet periods. It is a complete cooking and controlled cooling process; briefly warming cooked meat or toasting an assembled cold burger is insufficient.

This revision retains the original 20 core products and eight frozen formats, and adds four optional savoury products O-A01–O-A04. They are development options, not a recommendation to launch 32 products together. H01–H05 and this section govern preparation ahead wherever an older recipe refers to cooking, cooling, transport or reheating. All cooking times, tray loads and yields below require a small measured trial on the actual oven.

## Product-by-product service decision

| Product | What to prepare ahead | What happens when ordered or in a small service batch |
| --- | --- | --- |
| O-P13/O-P14 chicken/beef shawarma | Fully oven-cooked strips, cooled and portioned; bread, sauces and garnish stored separately | Reheat meat thoroughly, cook fries, assemble and toast bread briefly |
| O-P16 beef cheeseburger | Fully cooked individual patties, rapidly cooled separately | Reheat patty, melt cheese, toast bun, then add cold garnish and sauce |
| O-P15 crispy chicken burger; O-P17 chicken box | Chilled raw portions and separate coating; or fully cooked chilled chicken after a texture trial | Prefer a full fry to order; the alternative is one complete reheat in a dry oven or suitable fryer, with temperature and crispness checks |
| O-P06 fish roll; O-P07 beef pie; O-P09 chicken pie; O-P18 chicken samosa | Cooked chilled filling and separate dough; assemble small chilled batches | Final fry/bake heats the filling again: this is its one reheating step. Serve or hold hot; do not chill finished leftovers for another reheat |
| O-P08 sausage roll | Chilled raw sausage filling enclosed in pastry | Bake completely as the first cook; a finished roll can instead be cooled and reheated once under the documented route |
| O-P10 egg roll | Cooked chilled egg and separate dough | Final fry is the egg's reheating step; serve or hold hot, with no later cool-and-reheat cycle |
| O-P12 chicken pizza; O-A04 beef pizza | Chilled cooked topping, dough and sauce kept separate | Final pizza bake is the meat's reheating step; verify the topping temperature and serve |
| O-P19 loaded chicken fries | Chilled cooked chicken; sauces and pickles separate | Reheat chicken once, cook fries fresh, assemble immediately |
| O-A01 roast chicken burger | Oven-cooked chicken portion | Reheat, then assemble with toasted bun and chilled garnish |
| O-A02 fish burger; O-A03 fish wrap | Deboned raw chilled fish for cooking to order, or fully cooked chilled fish for the measured oven route | Cook or reheat as applicable, then assemble; avoid repeated handling of fragile fish |

The single-reheat house rule follows [UK food business guidance](https://www.gov.uk/food-hygiene-businesses/cooking). It is an operating choice for this plan, not a statement of Cameroon law. Track the heat history of the ingredient: putting reheated chicken into a different product does not reset its history. Prefer fully cooked patties and roast chicken for the initial prepare-ahead trial; compare fish and crispy products with freshly cooked controls before adoption.

## One controlled route from production to the customer

1. Receive sound ingredients and keep raw proteins chilled under H01–H05. Thaw covered in the refrigerator, below ready-to-eat food. Weigh raw portions with dedicated utensils; wash hands and clean/sanitize the area before handling cooked food. Marinade is not a preservation treatment. Discard raw-contact marinade after use; never use it as a finishing sauce.

2. Fully cook using the appropriate method below. Use the H01–H05 house cooking target throughout; check the thickest portions at the oven's slowest-heating positions. Record batch identity, time and measured food temperature. Browning is a quality cue, not evidence of core doneness. Never partly cook meat or chicken, refrigerate it, then finish later. [USDA guidance on partial cooking](https://ask.fsis.usda.gov/article/Is-it-safe-to-partially-cook-meat-or-poultry-and-finish-it-later)

3. Start active cooling immediately after cooking. Arrange patties or fillets in a single layer; spread strips/fillings in shallow food-safe pans with circulation around them. Use a blast chiller if available, or another method demonstrated to meet H01–H05. A large refrigerator/freezer does not establish sufficient cooling capacity. Protect loosely covered food during cooling, then cover fully when chilled. Log the cooling checkpoints; do not leave trays on the counter to reach room temperature. [FDA employee cooling guidance](https://www.fda.gov/media/181882/download)

4. Weigh chilled usable output with clean utensils, quickly enough to maintain temperature control. Portion into shallow labelled containers; do not pack hot food into stacked deep takeaway boxes. Label recipe, species, batch, cook time, cooling completion, weight, use-by and reheat count. Patties may be formed before cooking; weigh after chilling to check shrinkage. Discard bones, gristle and unsuitable pieces before calculating saleable yield.

5. Transport covered portions with sufficient ice packs in clean insulated carriers, or suitable refrigerated transport. Record dispatch and arrival food temperatures; maintain the H01–H05 cold limit during the journey and sales-site storage. Keep raw and cooked foods separate. Do not rely on journey length alone. Quarantine and discard food with an unknown unsafe temperature history; reheating cannot repair every form of temperature abuse.

6. Reheat only the ordered portion, directly from refrigeration, using a suitable induction pan with a lid, griddle or small oven. Reheat rapidly to the H01–H05 house target, confirmed at the thickest centre; a holding cabinet is not a reheater. Avoid using the large rotary oven for individual orders. Set a timed method only after testing multiple portions and starting temperatures. Add any measured drinking water early enough for the complete portion to reach the target; do not add cold juices afterwards.

7. Toast the bun or bread separately. Add the hot protein, then refrigerated sauce, washed garnish and drained pickles with clean utensils. Serve promptly. A brief final wrap toast is a bread-quality step after the meat is already safely reheated. Never prepare a stock of warm assembled burgers or wraps on the counter.

8. For a small rush, maintain finished hot protein under H01–H05; monitor actual food temperature. Use the report's two-hour hot-display quality target and reduce batches if drying occurs. Reheated unsold portions are discarded, not cooled again. Initial launch policy remains same-day preparation and sale with disposal at close. Do not infer a refrigerated multi-day shelf life from this process. Planned frozen production needs its own documented storage, thawing and quality trial before rollout; freezing must never be used to rescue mishandled food.

## Oven method for O-B18 chicken and O-B19 beef shawarma

Use the complete marinades already specified in O-B18/O-B19. For a pilot use their 1 kg raw boneless meat formulas; multiply every ingredient by five only after proving the smaller load. Bone-in purchasing requires an additional measured deboning yield. Food-grade solid roasting pans with raised edges are needed inside the 40 × 60 cm trays; do not let raw juices drip through racks. Do not bake bread or ready-to-eat pastries underneath or alongside raw proteins.

Chicken: use boneless thigh for a forgiving reheating trial, or test breast separately because it dries more easily. Cut even pieces about 15–20 mm thick, marinate covered under refrigeration for a trial 2–12 hours within the same-day workflow, and spread in one layer without crowding. Trial a preheated 190°C oven, checking early and typically around 15–25 minutes depending on cut and loading. Turn or redistribute only with raw/cooked utensil control. Release by the H01–H05 probe result, not time. A lightly browned surface with moist interior is the quality target. Slice using a clean knife after cooking, then cool promptly; include slicing time in the cooling record.

Beef: choose a tender boneless cut and slice across the grain into even thin strips. Use O-B19 marinade and a short refrigerated trial of 2–6 hours. Trial 190–200°C in a shallow single layer, starting checks around 8–15 minutes. The cook may be shorter or longer; do not hold it in the oven merely to reach an assumed time. Tough stew cuts are not interchangeable with tender strips: use a separately tested covered braise, then cool and portion the shredded result with a revised yield. Do not promise shawarma texture from an unidentified cheap cut.

The original O-B18/O-B19 targets of 750/700 g per 1 kg raw meat are initial cooking assumptions. For the new route, weigh output after cooling and again after reheating. Use only the final usable meat mass for the advertised serving. If the chilled yield matches those assumptions and reheating retains 95%, final yields become 712.5 g chicken and 665 g beef. Do not add raw marinade weight to saleable meat yield. Reheat with only a small measured splash of water when needed; avoid boiling strips in a large pool of liquid.

## Beef hamburger patties: oven, portion and finish

Keep O-B27's full formula: beef mince 1,000 g; salt 12 g; black pepper 3 g; paprika 3 g; oil allowance 10 g. Mix the mince and dry seasoning briefly; use the oil for pan release. The meat mixture is 1,018 g before the pan oil. Form ten equal patties approximately 102 g each, about 12–15 mm thick. Do not heavily compress or press them during cooking. Keep raw patties cold until loading.

Trial cook on lightly oiled raised-edge pans at 190°C, turning once if needed, with initial checks around 10–15 minutes. Probe sideways into the centre, reach H01–H05, then cool patties separately in one layer. This fully cooked oven route is an alternative to O-B27's fresh hot-plate method. At service, reheat the patty in a suitable pan/oven, check the centre, then melt the 20 g cheese portion and assemble O-P16. A quick surface sear alone does not adequately reheat the centre.

O-P16 promises 75 g final cooked beef. Its original 750 g batch assumes ten portions directly after cooking. At an illustrative 95% reheating retention, a 75 g chilled patty yields only 71.25 g after reheating. To preserve the 75 g final claim, target 78.95 g chilled per patty and scale the O-B27 batch by 1/0.95: beef 1,052.6 g; salt 12.63 g; pepper 3.16 g; paprika 3.16 g; oil 10.53 g. Form ten larger raw patties from that mixture and validate the final yield. Do not silently describe a smaller reheated portion as 75 g. Whole patties cannot be topped up neatly with scraps; adjust raw forming weight from measured losses.

An alternative is to chill safely prepared raw patties and cook fully to order. Compare that route with prepare-ahead patties for juiciness, waiting time, saleable yield and total cost. Never pre-brown raw patties for later completion.

## Crispy chicken, pies, rolls and samosas

For O-B26/O-P15/O-P17, the best starting quality trial is refrigerated raw marinated portions followed by coating and a full fry to order. Keep coating contaminated by raw chicken away from clean dry stock and discard the used portion. Do not partly fry chicken and store it for later finishing. A fully cooked, rapidly chilled portion may be reheated once in a dry oven or suitable fryer, but the centre must meet H01–H05; reject a method that burns the outside, dries the meat or softens the coating. Do not assume the existing 1,150 g coated yield remains unchanged after this extra process.

For O-P06/O-P07/O-P09/O-P18, cool the prepared filling before assembly. The final bake/fry reheats that filling. If production and sales are separate, either transport the finished product hot under control, or transport properly chilled assembled uncooked pastries for final cooking in suitable equipment at the sales site. The latter route needs validated same-day handling and final cooking capacity; no sales-site oven has been confirmed. If neither route is possible, postpone that product rather than chilling a finished twice-heated filling for a third heat cycle. O-P10 cooked egg follows the same principle.

O-P08 is different because O-B10 uses raw sausage meat. Baking the assembled sausage roll is its first complete cook; it may then be rapidly cooled and reheated once before sale, with both stages checked under H01–H05. Do not substitute an already cooked sausage without revising that history. Reheated pastry should be heated in an oven appropriate to the sales site for texture; a warm display unit cannot perform this step.

All new prices below are test assumptions in FCFA, not observed Nkambe prices or promised profit. Base cost includes all its ingredient inputs divided by usable yield. The additional 5% reheating loss is an illustrative planning allowance; replace it with measured chilled-to-final retention for each product. Prepared components in these ten-unit pilots are scaled proportionately; if staff make whole batches and discard balances, charge that extra waste separately.

| Product | Ingredients/unit | Pack | Other variable | Test price | Contribution | Margin |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| O-A01 Roast chicken burger | 917.7 | 60 | 100 | 2,200 | 1,122.3 | 51.0% |
| O-A02 Fish burger | 921.1 | 60 | 100 | 2,300 | 1,218.9 | 53.0% |
| O-A03 Fish wrap | 839.3 | 65 | 110 | 2,200 | 1,185.7 | 53.9% |
| O-A04 Beef mini pizza | 835.3 | 45 | 90 | 1,500 | 529.7 | 35.3% |

The variable allowance covers a provisional combined amount for cooking/reheating energy, cold handling, cleaning and incremental service work. It is not a measured quotation and excludes fixed payroll, rent, financing and taxes. Fixed overhead still has to be paid from contribution. Sales prices must be tested against portion size and local willingness to pay; the fish menu may be unsuitable at the assumed fillet price.

For the existing products, update rather than reuse the original margin unaltered. At unchanged final portions and 95% reheat retention, the protein ingredient surcharge alone is about **24.1 FCFA per O-P16 burger**, **36.6 per standard O-P13 chicken wrap**, **41.0 per standard O-P14 beef wrap**, and **26.6 per O-P19 loaded fries**. Add measured refrigeration, packaging, transport, energy and labour differences as well. These surcharges assume the original cooking yield equals the measured chilled yield; if it does not, recalculate from raw purchase weight. Pies/fillings and coated chicken need their own measured yields rather than a universal 5% deduction.

Cost per final kg of meat = total raw batch ingredient cost ÷ chilled usable kilograms ÷ reheating retention. Reheat retention = final edible weight ÷ chilled portion weight. Yield percentages multiply: chicken at 75% chilled yield and 95% reheat retention gives 71.25% final raw-to-service yield. Margin is not markup, and contribution is not net profit.

## Staff release sheet and equipment implications

| Record for every production batch | Entry needed |
| --- | --- |
| Identity and quantities | Date, recipe, supplier lot, raw kg, operator and intended products |
| Cooking | Oven/pan setting, tray positions, thickest-centre probe results and time |
| Cooling | Start and checkpoint times/temperatures against H01–H05; pan depth and batch size |
| Portioning | Chilled usable kg, portion g, number packed, rejects and label/use-by |
| Transfer | Dispatch and receipt times/food temperatures, carrier and receiving operator |
| Service | Reheat result, final portion weight, reheat count, orders sold and disposal |
| Quality/cost | Juiciness or crispness, assembly time, measured retention, energy and total unit cost |

Begin with one or two trays, map cooking and cooling performance, and increase only when the slowest portions meet the controls. The 16-tray oven's physical capacity is not evidence that refrigeration can safely cool 16 trays at once. Separate production of different species where needed for handling, allergens and temperature checks. Use oven-safe pans, cleaned probe thermometers, accurate scales, shallow cooling containers, insulated transport carriers and a suitable sales-site reheating pan or oven. Confirm what is already owned before buying anything.

Cooking in the diesel oven uses diesel plus its listed 3 kW electrical auxiliaries. Refrigeration after cooking and reheating at the sales point still consume power. The sales inverter remains 15 kW with 30 kWh nominal battery storage; replacing the 3.5 kW shawarma appliance with a 5 kW induction cooker can increase instantaneous demand if operated at full power. Rebuild the simultaneous-load schedule with both ice-cream machines, fryers and refrigeration before service. Keep cold storage supplied continuously; this workflow does not justify switching it off to run another appliance.

Launch acceptance: a documented safe process, a successful cold-transport trial, repeatable final weights, acceptable taste against fresh controls, and a revised contribution calculation using actual quotations and losses. If cooling capacity or service reheating fails, reduce the batch or cook fully to order. Do not extend storage time to make an oversized batch commercially convenient.

# 7. Frozen desserts and portion formats

Gravity machines need a smooth mix that flows reliably. Use the actual manufacturer’s approved range, hopper refrigeration requirement, minimum charge and cleaning instructions. The 1 kg formula is a composition pilot, not necessarily a sufficient operating charge. Separate hopper cooling and approved standby functions are model-specific; an unknown machine must not inherit another brand’s operating procedure. [Taylor gravity soft-serve example](https://www.taylor-company.com/equipment/equipment-detail/model-c161/)

For on-site dairy-mix processing, follow H02. Its batch reference is at least 69°C for 30 minutes throughout the mix in a qualified system. A continuous-process holding time cannot be transferred to an ordinary kettle. Use an approved ready mix if treatment and rapid chilling have not been demonstrated. [N04]

After a validated heat step, chill promptly under H01–H05 and target 1–4°C before aging/loading. Never put warm mix into the hopper. Keep pre-freeze mix covered and chilled, process it in small batches, and do not return dispensed ice cream to the hopper. Use the actual manual’s dismantling, detergent, sanitizer concentration/contact time, rinsing and lubrication directions. The safe default launch routine is fresh mix each day, no carryover/rerun, and daily dismantle-clean-sanitize unless a documented approved cycle explicitly differs.

For frozen stock, adopt a storage target of −18°C or colder and log temperatures. This is an operating target, not a validated shelf life; a storage freezer’s nameplate does not establish its ability to rapidly harden new bars. Do not assign a commercial life from a generic seven-day trial; validate the actual product, process and storage. Soft serve is dispensed for immediate consumption, not stored as prefilled cups at its serving temperature.

## Measuring overrun and mould volume

Overrun measures added volume, not extra mass. Use: overrun % = 100 × (weight of a fixed cup of liquid mix / weight of the same cup of frozen product − 1). At assumed mix density 1.09 kg/L and 30% overrun, frozen density is 1.09/1.30 = 0.838 kg/L. Measure rather than assuming either machine reaches that value. [University of Guelph overrun calculations](https://books.lib.uoguelph.ca/icecreamtechnologyebook/chapter/overrun-calculations/)

The owner confirms the large pop is twice the small one, but no absolute volume is known. Calibrate each mould with water to its intended fill line: tared water grams approximate ml. Repeat three times and average. Then weigh a known volume of actual mix for its density. Fill mass = working volume × mix density. Units = floor(usable mix mass / fill mass); remainder = usable mix mass − units × fill mass. Leave manufacturer-required expansion space; do not fill to the brim.

| Illustration only | Small | Large |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Working fill volume | 50 ml | 100 ml |
| Mix density assumed | 1.10 g/ml | 1.10 g/ml |
| Fill mass | 55 g | 110 g |
| Usable mass from 1 kg formula | 970 g | 970 g |
| Full units | 17 | 8 |
| Unallocated remainder charged to batch | 35 g | 90 g |

For a x5 batch, 4,850 g usable mix would fill 88 small or 44 large example moulds, leaving 10 g; it does not have to preserve the inefficient pilot’s 17/8 yields. The costing table deliberately uses conservative pilot counts. Recalculate production contribution using actual cavity count, startup residue and integer mould yields; do not mix the two bases when reporting profit.

## Frozen mix composition assumptions

For trial calculations, UHT whole milk is assumed 3.5% fat and 13% total solids; cream 35% fat and 40% solids; skim powder 97% solids; condensed milk uses the condensed-milk assumptions in chapter 4; glucose is dry glucose powder, not an 80% solids syrup. FBASE1 is approximately 6.2% fat and 35% total solids. These are engineering estimates to check against labels, not laboratory analyses or legal “ice cream” classifications.

Glucose form, fruit sugar, milk solids and stabilizer affect freezing and viscosity. Do not replace dry glucose with equal-weight syrup or omit stabilizer without rebalancing. FBASE3 uses pasteurized fruit ingredients and supplier-defined handling rather than borrowing a dairy pasteurization schedule. FBASE4’s yogurt and pasteurized puree are assembled hygienically cold; the final mix has no claimed kill step. Keep both out of an unapproved dairy hopper.

## O-FBASE1 Gravity soft serve vanilla trial mix

Pilot input 1,000 g; target usable output 970 g. Planning production batch is x5: input 5,000 g and usable output 4,850 g. These are trial targets; machine minimum/maximum batch capacities remain unverified.

| Ingredient or prepared component | Pilot g | Production g | Baker % |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| UHT milk | 660 | 3,300 | — |
| Cream 35% | 100 | 500 | — |
| Skim milk powder | 55 | 275 | — |
| Sugar | 100 | 500 | — |
| Glucose powder | 30 | 150 | — |
| Condensed milk | 50 | 250 | — |
| Ice cream stabilizer blend | 3 | 15 | — |
| Vanilla | 2 | 10 | — |

1. Confirm each machine model, feed system and permitted fat, solids, stabilizer and viscosity range before loading. The owner describes one machine as soft serve and the other as gravity; these categories overlap, so the second is provisionally treated as gravity-fed soft serve, with its actual product mode still to be verified. If either manual rejects this mix, use its approved commercial premix until a compatible reformulation is tested.

2. Dry-blend sugar, glucose powder, milk powder and an ice-cream-specific stabilizer/emulsifier blend. The 3 g dose is provisional: follow the actual blend label, offset any dose change with milk and recheck solids.

3. Warm milk and cream, whisk in dry blend, condensed milk and vanilla. Apply the controlled dairy processing protocol F0 before use. Reconstituted milk powder must not be treated as sterile.

4. Cool rapidly, keep covered at 1–4°C and age 4–8 hours as a texture trial if the stabilizer instructions require it. Load only sanitized equipment and cold mix; record input and output weights.

5. Freeze and dispense following the manufacturer’s start-up settings. Begin testing at 30% overrun as a calculation assumption, not a machine guarantee. Never add chunks, coarse nut paste or fruit fibre to a gravity feed hopper.

Yield and process note: 1,000 g mix input; 970 g saleable frozen mass after assumed 3% loss. Estimate 6.2% fat and 35% total solids using stated ingredient compositions. The real machine’s hold-up may exceed this allowance; a 1 kg pilot is a formulation trial, not necessarily a sufficient machine charge.

Illustrative ingredient cost: 2,100 FCFA per pilot batch; 2,164.9 FCFA/kg usable output. This excludes packaging and operating costs.

Pilot input 1,000 g; target usable output 970 g. Planning production batch is x5: input 5,000 g and usable output 4,850 g. These are trial targets; machine minimum/maximum batch capacities remain unverified.

| Ingredient or prepared component | Pilot g | Production g | Baker % |

| UHT milk | 640 | 3,200 | — |
