| TOTAL | 2,000 | 100.000% | 10,000 |

Mass route, pilot: 2,000 g input − 40 g handling/unretained (2%) − 0 g process loss (0% of post-handling mass) = 1,960 g retained. Allocation: 78.4 g finished component per unit. Production retained mass: 9,800 g. All losses are unmeasured planning allowances.

### Batch yield and portion control

Total ingredient input 2,000 g; modeled losses 40 g; net edible output 1,960 g = 25 × 78.4 g expected finished units, with no modeled remainder. Production example: 10,000 g input → 9,800 g output across 125 units. Display rounding is not label-weight verification.

### Composition and freezing/fermentation controls

Development composition estimate (combined input before processing): fat 0.00%, milk solids-not-fat 0.00%, total solids 21.85%, unfermented lactose 0.00%; added sucrose 19.00% and anhydrous dextrose 2.50%. These are formulation estimates, not nutrition analysis or a legal identity certificate.

Freezing comparison only: sucrose-equivalent added-sugar index = sucrose% + 1.9 × anhydrous dextrose% = 23.75. This is NOT a full PAC/freezing-point prediction: native fruit sugars, lactose, salts, actual solids and overrun also matter. Confirm bite/draw at the real freezer temperatures; never adjust sugar without recalculating solids and rerunning the freeze test.

### Numbered production method

1. For the infusion process input, use 30 g food-grade dried hibiscus and 1,600 g potable water. Boil/simmer as a flavour extraction trial for 5–10 minutes, strain hygienically and weigh 1,500 g infusion. Record retained liquid, spent solids and vapor separately; extraction alone is not a validated commercial kill step.
2. Use only a validated treated infusion for commercial manufacture. Cost its actual dry flowers, water, labor and extraction losses as one input; never treat 1,500 g infusion as 1,500 g dry hibiscus.
3. Premix stabilizer and sugar, combine with all liquids and hydrate according to H03. Verify a suitable whole-mix treatment with the process specialist. Measure pH and °Brix, initially about 21.5% added sugars before native extract solids.
4. Cool rapidly to 4°C or below, rest 2–4 hours covered and fill molds by mass. Freeze and demold using the machine-specific checks in S-I01.
5. Wrap against dehydration, record stick and film lots and store at -18°C or colder. Compare ginger heat and acidity with customers in a controlled trial.

### Quality, defects and controlled variants

Weak color → old flowers/dilution. Harsh astringency → too much extraction. Soft stick → sugar excess. Ice crystals → slow freeze/storage cycles.

No claim that acidity eliminates pathogens. A ready pasteurized hibiscus base is a lower-complexity commissioning option if documented and affordable.

### Allergens, packing, life study and costing

Allergens/ingredients to declare: No major allergen intentionally added; verify all labels and shared equipment.

Wrap sticks/bars in freezer-grade film or lid scoop tubs immediately, with batch/net mass/allergens. Store and distribute at -18°C or colder under measured control. No reheating, thaw/refreeze sales or assumed month-long life. Study days 0/7/14/28, crystal size, melt, seal and microbial plan under H05.

## S-I03 — Cocoa milk popsicle

25 × 78.4 g modeled sticks, no overrun intended.

Stage: Cold-line phase • Fit class 3 • Pilot 25 units • Production example ×5 = 125 units.

Commercial case: Lower equipment complexity than aerated coated bars once the cold chain exists. Cocoa provenance and price require supplier evidence.

Technical basis: [S09], [S14]. All exact formulas and operating adaptations here require controlled trials.

### Formula — Cocoa mix

Percent basis: this component input = 100% (not whole assembled product).

| Ingredient / specification | Pilot g | Percent | Production g |
|---|---|---|---|
| Whole milk 3.5% | 1,450 | 72.500% | 7,250 |
| Water | 150 | 7.500% | 750 |
| Sugar | 270 | 13.500% | 1,350 |
| Skim milk powder | 60 | 3.000% | 300 |
| Cocoa powder | 60 | 3.000% | 300 |
| STAB 2000 | 8 | 0.400% | 40 |
| Vanilla | 2 | 0.100% | 10 |
| TOTAL | 2,000 | 100.000% | 10,000 |

Mass route, pilot: 2,000 g input − 40 g handling/unretained (2%) − 0 g process loss (0% of post-handling mass) = 1,960 g retained. Allocation: 78.4 g finished component per unit. Production retained mass: 9,800 g. All losses are unmeasured planning allowances.

### Batch yield and portion control

Total ingredient input 2,000 g; modeled losses 40 g; net edible output 1,960 g = 25 × 78.4 g expected finished units, with no modeled remainder. Production example: 10,000 g input → 9,800 g output across 125 units. Display rounding is not label-weight verification.

### Composition and freezing/fermentation controls

Development composition estimate (combined input before processing): fat 2.90%, milk solids-not-fat 9.38%, total solids 28.75%, unfermented lactose 5.04%; added sucrose 13.50% and anhydrous dextrose 0.00%. These are formulation estimates, not nutrition analysis or a legal identity certificate.

Freezing comparison only: sucrose-equivalent added-sugar index = sucrose% + 1.9 × anhydrous dextrose% = 13.50. This is NOT a full PAC/freezing-point prediction: native fruit sugars, lactose, salts, actual solids and overrun also matter. Confirm bite/draw at the real freezer temperatures; never adjust sugar without recalculating solids and rerunning the freeze test.

### Numbered production method

1. Use fine unsweetened cocoa with stated fat content and pasteurized/UHT milk. Premix cocoa, sugar, milk powder and STAB 2000, then disperse completely with the liquids.
2. Apply H02 validated mix heating/cooling. A stick blender can disperse particles but is not a pressure homogenizer. Check the bottom for scorching.
3. Age covered 4–12 hours at 2–4°C; reblend gently. Fill clean molds by mass, leaving tested headspace and inserting centered sticks.
4. Freeze by actual machine endpoint, not merely elapsed time. Demold fully hardened sticks, wrap promptly and store at -18°C or colder.
5. Assess bite from the actual freezer: target firm but not tooth-hard. Measure top/bottom cocoa uniformity and no gritty sediment.

### Quality, defects and controlled variants

Grit → poor dispersion/cocoa grade. Sediment → insufficient hydration. Ice hardness → sugar/solids imbalance. Gumminess → excessive stabilizer.

Do not replace 8 g STAB with 8 g starch; they are different systems. Coconut milk version requires separate fat/emulsion balance and label.

### Allergens, packing, life study and costing

Allergens/ingredients to declare: milk.

Wrap sticks/bars in freezer-grade film or lid scoop tubs immediately, with batch/net mass/allergens. Store and distribute at -18°C or colder under measured control. No reheating, thaw/refreeze sales or assumed month-long life. Study days 0/7/14/28, crystal size, melt, seal and microbial plan under H05.

## S-I04 — Own-brand premium chocolate-coated ice-cream bar

28 bars: 77 g center + 13.5 g shell = 90.5 g theoretical net. No external brand/logo reproduced.

Stage: Investment phase • Fit class 3 • Pilot 28 units • Production example ×5 = 140 units.

Commercial case: Gift/treat premium hypothesis; cocoa-butter coating and frozen distribution may dominate cost. Preorders first, no impulse-volume promise.

Technical basis: [S09], [S13], [S16], [S17]. All exact formulas and operating adaptations here require controlled trials.

### Formula — Dairy mix

Percent basis: this component input = 100% (not whole assembled product).

| Ingredient / specification | Pilot g | Percent | Production g |
|---|---|---|---|
| Whole milk 3.5% | 1,170 | 53.182% | 5,850 |
| Cream 35% | 520 | 23.636% | 2,600 |
| Skim milk powder | 90 | 4.091% | 450 |
| Sugar | 280 | 12.727% | 1,400 |
| Dextrose anhydrous | 60 | 2.727% | 300 |
| STAB 2000 | 6 | 0.273% | 30 |
| Vanilla | 8 | 0.364% | 40 |
| Water | 66 | 3.000% | 330 |
| TOTAL | 2,200 | 100.000% | 11,000 |

Mass route, pilot: 2,200 g input − 44 g handling/unretained (2%) − 0 g process loss (0% of post-handling mass) = 2,156 g retained. Allocation: 77 g finished component per unit. Production retained mass: 10,780 g. All losses are unmeasured planning allowances.

### Formula — Ice-chocolate shell

Percent basis: this component input = 100% (not whole assembled product).

| Ingredient / specification | Pilot g | Percent | Production g |
|---|---|---|---|
| Callebaut Ice Chocolate White | 420 | 100.000% | 2,100 |
| TOTAL | 420 | 100.000% | 2,100 |

Mass route, pilot: 420 g input − 42 g handling/unretained (10%) − 0 g process loss (0% of post-handling mass) = 378 g retained. Allocation: 13.5 g finished component per unit. Production retained mass: 1,890 g. All losses are unmeasured planning allowances.

### Batch yield and portion control

Total ingredient input 2,620 g; modeled losses 86 g; net edible output 2,534 g = 28 × 90.5 g expected finished units, with no modeled remainder. Production example: 13,100 g input → 12,670 g output across 140 units. Display rounding is not label-weight verification.

### Composition and freezing/fermentation controls

Development composition estimate (center only; coating excluded): fat 10.17%, milk solids-not-fat 10.04%, total solids 35.95%, unfermented lactose 5.41%; added sucrose 12.73% and anhydrous dextrose 2.73%. These are formulation estimates, not nutrition analysis or a legal identity certificate.

Freezing comparison only: sucrose-equivalent added-sugar index = sucrose% + 1.9 × anhydrous dextrose% = 17.91. This is NOT a full PAC/freezing-point prediction: native fruit sugars, lactose, salts, actual solids and overrun also matter. Confirm bite/draw at the real freezer temperatures; never adjust sugar without recalculating solids and rerunning the freeze test.

### Numbered production method

1. Make the complete S-D02 center mix under H02, batch-freeze at a controlled low overrun (trial 20–30%) and immediately fill sanitized bar molds by mass.
2. Allocate the usable center evenly to 28 molds; insert sticks to a reproducible depth with at least roughly two-thirds of center length supported. Actual mold geometry must permit this without protrusion.
3. Harden to a measured core of -18°C or colder in a rated hardener. Demold small batches and keep remaining bars frozen; ordinary popsicle equipment is not assumed to aerate/fill this center.
4. Melt the specified Callebaut Ice Chocolate White to 35–40°C according to its product guidance. It is a special ice coating; no conventional tempering is required for this product. Keep water/condensation out.
5. Dip briefly, drain and weigh shell pickup. Model 420 g working shell input, 42 g unretained/unusable and 378 g on bars (13.5 g each). A deeper dip vessel may require extra reusable working stock, tracked separately as inventory.
6. Let the shell set while the core remains frozen, wrap individually in freezer-grade film and return immediately to -18°C or colder. Check stick pullout, shell cracks and package seal.
7. Do not use Magnum branding or imply its proprietary recipe. A own-brand dark/compound version needs its exact supplier’s melt instructions, label, pickup and allergen trial.

### Quality, defects and controlled variants

Thick shell → cold/high-viscosity coating. Sliding shell → surface ice/condensation. Cracks → warm core or thermal shock. Loose stick → incomplete hardening.

Ordinary couverture follows its own tempering curve; compound coating follows its fat-system instructions. Neither can inherit this special ice-chocolate temperature blindly.

### Allergens, packing, life study and costing

Allergens/ingredients to declare: milk.

Wrap sticks/bars in freezer-grade film or lid scoop tubs immediately, with batch/net mass/allergens. Store and distribute at -18°C or colder under measured control. No reheating, thaw/refreeze sales or assumed month-long life. Study days 0/7/14/28, crystal size, melt, seal and microbial plan under H05.

## S-Y01 — Plain set yogurt

20 cups, theoretical 104.174 g net each. Package by verified net mass; do not imply 100 ml equals 100 g.

Stage: Cold-line phase • Fit class 3 • Pilot 20 units • Production example ×10 = 200 units.

Commercial case: Plain yogurt supports family breakfasts and ingredient use; no probiotic/health claim. A 20 L milk run is conditional, not a confirmed 50 L vessel load.

Technical basis: [S18], [S19], [S20]. All exact formulas and operating adaptations here require controlled trials.

### Formula — Yogurt base

Percent basis: this component input = 100% (not whole assembled product).

| Ingredient / specification | Pilot g | Percent | Production g |
|---|---|---|---|
| Whole milk 3.5% | 2,060 | 96.896% | 20,600 |
| Skim milk powder | 60 | 2.822% | 600 |
| Yogourmet Original 3g sachet | 6 | 0.282% | 60 |
| TOTAL | 2,126 | 100.000% | 21,260 |

Mass route, pilot: 2,126 g input − 42.52 g handling/unretained (2%) − 0 g process loss (0% of post-handling mass) = 2,083.48 g retained. Allocation: 104.174 g finished component per unit. Production retained mass: 20,834.8 g. All losses are unmeasured planning allowances.

### Batch yield and portion control

Total ingredient input 2,126 g; modeled losses 42.52 g; net edible output 2,083.48 g = 20 × 104.174 g expected finished units, with no modeled remainder. Production example: 21,260 g input → 20,834.8 g output across 200 units. Display rounding is not label-weight verification.

### Composition and freezing/fermentation controls

Development composition estimate (combined input before processing): fat 3.42%, milk solids-not-fat 11.40%, total solids 15.10%, unfermented lactose 6.12%; added sucrose 0.00% and anhydrous dextrose 0.00%. These are formulation estimates, not nutrition analysis or a legal identity certificate.

Lactose is the pre-fermentation estimate; acidification changes sugars/acids. These pilot formulas intentionally use no added stabilizer. Culture performance, pH curve and separation are checked, not assumed from a yogurt name.

### Numbered production method

1. Use documented homogenized pasteurized/UHT whole milk and skim milk powder. Milk basis is 2 L assumed at 1.030 kg/L, giving 2,060 g; verify actual density and sachet label.
2. Disperse milk powder without foam and heat under H04 to 90–95°C for 5 minutes throughout as a technical yogurt-milk treatment. Validate cold point and covered evaporation; this is not proof from the stirrer display.
3. Cool promptly to 42–44°C, add two complete current 3 g Yogourmet Original sachets, dispersing in a little treated milk then mixing back. Do not use old 5 g-pack instructions or backslop retail yogurt.
4. Fill sanitized food cups and cover before incubation. Incubate undisturbed at 42–44°C; take hygienic sample cups for pH measurement. Start checking after 3 hours; about 5 hours is a supplier timing guide, not release criteria.
5. End incubation at pH 4.5 or the qualified culture-specific endpoint; initiate rapid cooling. H04 requires monitored cooling to 4°C or below in a mapped small-cup process.
6. Refrigerate and retain samples; do not ship at ambient temperature. Verify pH again after cooling, clean mild aroma and intact gel without whey pooling.

### Quality, defects and controlled variants

No acidification → inactive culture/inhibitors/wrong temperature: quarantine, do not extend indefinitely. Whey → excessive heat movement/overacidification. Grainy → solids poor dispersion.

Commercial DVS culture may be more economical at scale, but choose a named supplier with units-per-batch instructions and rewrite the dose; no gram-for-gram replacement.

### Allergens, packing, life study and costing

Allergens/ingredients to declare: milk.

Seal in sanitized food-grade cups/bottles with tamper evidence; transport at 4°C or below with logs. No reheating for ordinary yogurt. Study days 0/1/3/7 with a qualified laboratory, not an assigned 7-day sale life. Do not taste gas-producing or suspect samples.

Culture check: the retained 6 g entry assumes two 3 g sachets for 2 L milk. Verify sachet mass and current regional supplier instructions before manufacture; a different culture is not substituted gram for gram.

## S-Y02 — Stirred mango yogurt

Twenty stirred fruit cups; fruit treatment is an explicit gate. Model fruit evaporation as zero in covered processing; measure and revise.

Stage: Cold-line phase • Fit class 3 • Pilot 20 units • Production example ×10 = 200 units.

Commercial case: Fruit sweetness may widen appeal; compare plain and mango in paid trials. Fruit availability, imported-puree cost and cold-chain supply are unresolved.

Technical basis: [S18], [S19], [S20], [S24]. All exact formulas and operating adaptations here require controlled trials.

### Formula — Yogurt base

Percent basis: this component input = 100% (not whole assembled product).

| Ingredient / specification | Pilot g | Percent | Production g |
|---|---|---|---|
| Whole milk 3.5% | 2,060 | 92.543% | 20,600 |
| Skim milk powder | 60 | 2.695% | 600 |
| Yogourmet Original 3g sachet | 6 | 0.270% | 60 |
| Sugar | 100 | 4.492% | 1,000 |
| TOTAL | 2,226 | 100.000% | 22,260 |

Mass route, pilot: 2,226 g input − 44.52 g handling/unretained (2%) − 0 g process loss (0% of post-handling mass) = 2,181.48 g retained. Allocation: 109.074 g finished component per unit. Production retained mass: 21,814.8 g. All losses are unmeasured planning allowances.

### Formula — Fruit preparation

Percent basis: this component input = 100% (not whole assembled product).

| Ingredient / specification | Pilot g | Percent | Production g |
|---|---|---|---|
| Pasteurized mango puree 15 Brix | 200 | 71.429% | 2,000 |
| Sugar | 80 | 28.571% | 800 |
| TOTAL | 280 | 100.000% | 2,800 |

Mass route, pilot: 280 g input − 0 g handling/unretained (0%) − 0 g process loss (0% of post-handling mass) = 280 g retained. Allocation: 14 g finished component per unit. Production retained mass: 2,800 g. All losses are unmeasured planning allowances.

### Batch yield and portion control

Total ingredient input 2,506 g; modeled losses 44.52 g; net edible output 2,461.48 g = 20 × 123.074 g expected finished units, with no modeled remainder. Production example: 25,060 g input → 24,614.8 g output across 200 units. Display rounding is not label-weight verification.

### Composition and freezing/fermentation controls

Development composition estimate (combined input before processing): fat 2.90%, milk solids-not-fat 9.67%, total solids 21.19%, unfermented lactose 5.19%; added sucrose 7.18% and anhydrous dextrose 0.00%. These are formulation estimates, not nutrition analysis or a legal identity certificate.

Lactose is the pre-fermentation estimate; acidification changes sugars/acids. These pilot formulas intentionally use no added stabilizer. Culture performance, pH curve and separation are checked, not assumed from a yogurt name.

### Numbered production method

1. Make the listed sweet yogurt base with S-Y01 heating and culture controls, but incubate in a sanitized covered fermentation vessel rather than final cups. Obtain supplier confirmation for the sweetened, fortified adaptation.
2. Ferment at 42–44°C and measure pH in hygienically drawn samples. Stop at pH 4.5 by prompt cooling; gently break the gel, avoiding froth.
3. Use an aseptic ready-to-eat mango puree. Dissolve the fruit-stage sugar hygienically in a portion of heated puree and apply a separately validated fruit preparation treatment; do not add unvalidated raw fruit after fermentation.
4. Cool fruit preparation to 4°C or below. Gently blend all 280 g into the cooled yogurt base; record loss, pH and fruit distribution. Do not dilute to replace missing dairy yield.
5. Fill sanitized cups, seal and keep at 4°C or below. Run pH, yeast/mold and sensory trials before setting any commercial storage life.

### Quality, defects and controlled variants

Whey separation → overacidification/aggressive stirring. Fermented fruit odor/gas → contamination: reject and investigate. Uneven color → poor gentle blending.

Pineapple is not a direct swap: fruit enzyme, acidity and heat treatment differ. Use separately treated fruit and rebalance.

### Allergens, packing, life study and costing

Allergens/ingredients to declare: milk.

Seal in sanitized food-grade cups/bottles with tamper evidence; transport at 4°C or below with logs. No reheating for ordinary yogurt. Study days 0/1/3/7 with a qualified laboratory, not an assigned 7-day sale life. Do not taste gas-producing or suspect samples.

Culture check: the retained 6 g entry assumes two 3 g sachets for 2 L milk. Verify sachet mass and current regional supplier instructions before manufacture; a different culture is not substituted gram for gram.

## S-Y03 — Vanilla drinking yogurt

Ten bottles, theoretical 216.58 g each. Bottle capacity needs headspace and measured density.

Stage: Cold-line phase • Fit class 3 • Pilot 10 units • Production example ×10 = 100 units.

Commercial case: Portable breakfast drink; compete on taste/portion/handling, not unsupported gut-health claims. S29 kossam is adjacent evidence, not identical product identity.

Technical basis: [S18], [S19], [S20], [S29]. All exact formulas and operating adaptations here require controlled trials.

### Formula — Yogurt base

Percent basis: this component input = 100% (not whole assembled product).

| Ingredient / specification | Pilot g | Percent | Production g |
|---|---|---|---|
| Whole milk 3.5% | 2,060 | 93.213% | 20,600 |
| Yogourmet Original 3g sachet | 6 | 0.271% | 60 |
| Sugar | 140 | 6.335% | 1,400 |
| Vanilla | 4 | 0.181% | 40 |
| TOTAL | 2,210 | 100.000% | 22,100 |

Mass route, pilot: 2,210 g input − 44.2 g handling/unretained (2%) − 0 g process loss (0% of post-handling mass) = 2,165.8 g retained. Allocation: 216.58 g finished component per unit. Production retained mass: 21,658 g. All losses are unmeasured planning allowances.

### Batch yield and portion control

Total ingredient input 2,210 g; modeled losses 44.2 g; net edible output 2,165.8 g = 10 × 216.58 g expected finished units, with no modeled remainder. Production example: 22,100 g input → 21,658 g output across 100 units. Display rounding is not label-weight verification.

### Composition and freezing/fermentation controls

Development composition estimate (combined input before processing): fat 3.26%, milk solids-not-fat 8.39%, total solids 18.26%, unfermented lactose 4.47%; added sucrose 6.33% and anhydrous dextrose 0.00%. These are formulation estimates, not nutrition analysis or a legal identity certificate.

Lactose is the pre-fermentation estimate; acidification changes sugars/acids. These pilot formulas intentionally use no added stabilizer. Culture performance, pH curve and separation are checked, not assumed from a yogurt name.

### Numbered production method

1. Prepare the listed milk, sugar and vanilla using H04 heat treatment; the base deliberately omits added milk powder to reduce body, not milk safety controls.
2. Cool to 42–44°C and add two current 3 g Original sachets per 2 L milk basis. Confirm this sweetened use with supplier; do not split industrial culture sachets without dosing instructions.
3. Incubate in a covered sanitary vessel; track pH and temperature. Stop at pH 4.5 with prompt cooling, then shear gently with the cleaned stick blender in short pulses until pourable.
4. Do not add raw water after fermentation. Fill ten sanitized food bottles by measured net mass and seal with tamper evidence; keep at 4°C or below.
5. Check pourability and separation after 24 and 48 hours, plus pH and laboratory plan. A “shake gently” instruction may address natural separation but cannot excuse microbial gas, spoilage or unsafe storage.

### Quality, defects and controlled variants

Too thick → culture/body system or inadequate controlled shear. Sediment/whey → weak stability. Swollen bottle → reject, no tasting; investigate hygiene.

Do not add a random pectin/CMC dose. A stabilized long-distribution version needs a selected acid-dairy stabilizer, supplier process and a new formula.

### Allergens, packing, life study and costing

Allergens/ingredients to declare: milk.

Seal in sanitized food-grade cups/bottles with tamper evidence; transport at 4°C or below with logs. No reheating for ordinary yogurt. Study days 0/1/3/7 with a qualified laboratory, not an assigned 7-day sale life. Do not taste gas-producing or suspect samples.

Culture check: the retained 6 g entry assumes two 3 g sachets for 2 L milk. Verify sachet mass and current regional supplier instructions before manufacture; a different culture is not substituted gram for gram.

# 9. Fruit juice and yogurt preservation

For the first commercial trials, use documented treated juice or purée and pasteurized/UHT dairy, make modest quantities, pack hygienically and maintain the cold chain. Add a validated in-house treatment only when the equipment and process have been qualified. The best method depends on the product, pack, sales rate, power reliability and desired life.

## Fruit juices: select the preservation route

| Route | Practical use | Conditions and limits |
|---|---|---|
| Refrigerated treated juice | Small daily batches; immediate-service drinks | Use treated inputs or a validated final process, hygienic filling and storage at 4°C or below. Refrigeration slows growth; it does not make untreated juice safe. |
| Pasteurization followed by chilling | A repeatable packaged juice line | Have a specialist establish treatment for the exact juice, pH, soluble solids, pulp, batch size and equipment. Record the coldest product temperature and hold, then cool and fill under the approved procedure. |
| Freezing | Seasonal fruit purchasing and controlled stock | Use a safe treated product, portion into freezer-compatible containers with expansion space, freeze promptly and hold at −18°C or colder. Thaw under refrigeration. Freezing is not a kill step. |
| Contract high-pressure processing | A potential later fresh-taste line | Assess an available processor, validated product/package compatibility, transport and cold chain. Do not assume ambient stability or local availability. |
| Validated hot-fill or aseptic processing | A later product designed for ambient distribution | Requires a product-specific scheduled process and compatible package/closure. A sealed bottle, hot filling alone or added lemon does not establish shelf stability. |

Untreated juice has caused illness. Wash sound whole fruit under potable running water before cutting, remove damaged areas and reject rotten fruit; cleaning is not a substitute for an effective treatment. Do not use soap on fruit. Keep raw fruit handling separate from treated juice and sanitized bottles. [N01]

There is no one safe time–temperature recipe for all pineapple, mango, citrus, avocado blends or hibiscus drinks. Acidity, solids and pulp change the process. FDA’s five-log pathogen-reduction framework is a useful technical benchmark from the United States, not a claim about Cameroon’s legal requirements. Have the actual product and process assessed. [N02]

For freezing, use containers intended for the freezer, allow appropriate expansion headspace and label each portion. Freezing preserves stock but does not sterilize it. Use potable ice only if it is a declared formula ingredient; adding ice or water after treatment can recontaminate the drink and changes the formula. [N03]

## A practical juice production sequence

1. Define one product: fruit variety, supplier, juice/purée proportion, pulp size, water, sugar and all other ingredients. Record fruit condition and lot. Keep pineapple juice, mango nectar and avocado blends as separate specifications.
2. Measure and record pH and °Brix with calibrated instruments. These characterize the batch; neither reading alone proves safety. Record the measurement temperature and instrument ID.
3. Use the established treatment for the whole product, or documented treated inputs under an approved hygienic assembly process. Do not borrow a dairy heating schedule for juice.
4. Clean and sanitize bottles, closures, tools and contact surfaces using their supplier instructions. Use packaging suited to the actual fill temperature and cooling method. Record closure/seal checks.
5. Chill through the qualified process, keep at 4°C or below, and transport in monitored insulated/refrigerated equipment. Put out small service quantities and replace them with fresh sealed stock; do not top up a partly used dispenser.
6. Assign the production lot and the storage instruction immediately. Set the use-by period only from a product-specific study that includes the intended distribution and foreseeable temperature variation.

Lemon, ginger, sugar, ascorbic acid and stabilizers may affect flavour, colour, acidity or texture. They do not independently validate preservation. Antioxidants that reduce browning do not make avocado or banana purée shelf-stable. Do not improvise preservative doses; any future additive system needs an identified food-grade ingredient, a permitted use, supplier specification and a validated formulation.

## Yogurt: preserve the fermented product

| Method | Best use | Key control |
|---|---|---|
| Controlled fermentation, rapid cooling and sealed chilled storage | Set, stirred and drinking yogurt | Follow H04, the actual culture instructions and a verified acidification curve; cool promptly and maintain 4°C or below. |
| Small individual cups or bottles | Retail and transport | Reduces repeated opening and portion handling; validate sealing, cleanliness and cooling of the filled pack. |
| Separate treated fruit preparation | Mango yogurt and fruit layers | Cool the fruit preparation hygienically, combine under a controlled process and reassess the final product’s pH and life. |
| Freezing | Frozen-yogurt bars, smoothies or baking ingredients | Freeze only sound, safely handled product. Expect texture separation on thawing; freezing does not rescue spoiled yogurt. |
| Heat treatment after fermentation | A different, professionally developed product | Changes live-culture status and texture. Ambient stability requires a validated process and package; it is not achieved by ordinary reheating and bottling. |

For the three yogurt recipes S-Y01–S-Y03, use the shared milk-treatment, culture and cooling plan in H04. The retained 6 g starter amount assumes a particular sachet mass. The current Yogourmet page specifies one sachet per litre and 42–44°C incubation; verify the actual sachet and regional instructions. Do not confuse the dry starter’s shelf life with finished yogurt’s life. [N05]

The source’s pH 4.5 cooling trigger is a development target. Record the time taken to reach it, not simply an overnight incubation time. A failed acidification curve must be investigated; adding more culture late or leaving the batch warm indefinitely is not an approved recovery. A fermented product still needs hygienic handling, cooling and storage. The heat-treatment and fermentation stages work together. [N06]

For set yogurt, ferment in the final suitable cups and minimize disturbance of the gel. For stirred yogurt, use a controlled stirring step after fermentation. For drinking yogurt, adjust the formulation deliberately rather than diluting finished yogurt with untreated water. Add treated fruit or flavouring with sanitized tools and record the lot and amount. Sugar and thickener improve formulation properties; they do not replace refrigeration.

Keep yogurt covered and use clean serving tools. Discard the entire container if mould develops. Freezing can change yogurt’s texture, making it more suitable for smoothies or baking than for a smooth spoonable product after thawing. Assess your actual product before choosing that route. [N07]

## Power loss, transport and service

Place independent thermometers or loggers in storage and transport. Keep doors closed during interruptions, record the start time and temperatures, and prioritize cold storage in the power plan. Do not judge safety by taste or smell. Isolate stock with unknown temperature history and obtain a disposition under the food-safety plan.

Use 4°C or below as the house refrigeration target. FDA consumer guidance uses a two-hour limit for perishables at room temperature, reduced to one hour above 32°C; this is an action threshold, not a validated commercial shelf life or a substitute for logged cold-chain control. Keep service quantities small and minimize exposure. [N09]

## Preservation trial and release sheet

| Record | Required observation |
|---|---|
| Product identity | Recipe version, fruit/milk/culture lots, ingredients and actual masses |
| Process specification | Equipment, batch size, treatment program or treated-input evidence |
| Measurements | pH, °Brix where relevant, temperature/hold readings and calibrated instrument IDs |
| Yogurt fermentation | Inoculation temperature, culture identity/dose, timed pH readings, endpoint |
| Cooling and filling | Start/end times, product temperatures, container type, seal result |
| Storage and delivery | Continuous or scheduled temperature records, outages, dispatch/receipt |
| Shelf-life study | Agreed sampling dates, microbiological assessment, sensory/physical changes, packaging integrity |
| Decision | Approved process and life, rejected lot or further trial, reviewer and date |

Start with three repeat process trials before a broader life study. Agree microbiological testing and acceptance criteria with a competent food laboratory for the product. Test the finished packed product, not just incoming milk or fruit. Include the expected transport conditions. No automatic 7-, 14-, 30- or 90-day selling period is assigned here. Sampling on days 0, 1, 3 and 7 is a study design option, not permission to sell through day 7.

Preservation should support flavour as well as safety: compare aroma, colour, separation, texture and customer acceptance while recording refrigeration, packaging and spoilage cost per sold portion. This is especially useful when deciding whether to sell chilled juice, frozen juice portions or yogurt bars from the same seasonal fruit supply.

# 10. Cake-donut cracking: your controlled trial

## T01 — Rolled and cut cake donuts

Source: your September discussion “Reduce cracks in cake donuts”. These are your formula quantities, not a new validated commercial recipe.

| Ingredient | Original batch g | 1 kg flour pilot g |
|---|---|---|
| Flour | 6,000 | 1,000 |
| Sugar | 1,000 | 166.67 |
| Butter | 1,000 | 166.67 |
| Baking powder | 80 | 13.33 |
| Egg, edible mass | 1,500 | 250 |
| Water | 1,500 | 250 |
| EDC, identity to confirm | 10 | 1.67 |
| Total | 11,090 | 1,848.34 rounded |

EDC was described as viscous, but its identity, concentration and supplier dose were not confirmed. Do not assume EDC-95 or increase its dose. Obtain the label before continued use; if it cannot be identified, conduct the development control without it and document the change.

1. Record the existing mixing sequence, flour type, dough temperature, rolling thickness, cutter size and piece mass. Hold these constant between comparable tests.
2. Mix briefly after flour addition. Rest covered about 10 minutes as a trial, use minimal bench flour and avoid repeated rolling of scraps.
3. Compare a control at 250 g water per 1 kg flour with 275 g only if the control is stiff or tears. Keep all other identified ingredients unchanged. If already soft, do not add more water simply to remove cracks.
4. Run any EDC omission comparison separately from the water change. One variable at a time makes the result interpretable.
5. Trial frying at 180–185°C with measured oil recovery and a small load. Let the first surface set before turning; determine cook time using actual thickness and a set center. Oil temperature alone is not proof of doneness.
6. Cool samples and compare cracks, greasy texture, interior moisture, handling breakage and eating quality. Record at least ten pieces when possible. Select the formulation from repeated results, not a single attractive piece.

No portion count, rolling thickness, frying duration or shelf life was established in the discussion. Set these during the trial before issuing a production card. Declare wheat, egg and milk plus any emulsifier-carrier allergens.

# 11. Earlier formulas and development variants

These development records preserve distinct products and important formula differences from earlier drafts. They are not production instructions. Choose a complete primary card where available; otherwise resolve missing specifications, make a small controlled trial, measure yield and issue a full approved card. Original units and ranges remain visible so uncertainty is not hidden by invented conversions.

Older versions of the same product have been consolidated by family. Exact duplicates and superseded generic guidance are accounted for in the editorial record. Marketing claims about months of life, guaranteed profit, zero fuel cost or machinery performance have not been carried into working instructions.

## V01 — Agege bread and tangzhong milk bread

Source SRC03/SRC04. Agege, flour basis: flour 100%; water 55–58%; sugar 10–12%; fat 8–10%; instant yeast 1.5–2%; salt 1.5%; improver 0.5% as an unverified original entry. Use the actual improver supplier dose, not the generic percentage. Original starting method: mix 3 minutes low, 8–10 minutes faster; proof to expansion; bake near 180°C for 25–30 minutes. Piece mass, tin size, dough temperature and finished yield are missing. Retain the dense, stretchy style as a separate trial from S-B02.

Shokupan original: tangzhong flour 7% plus liquid 35%; main dough flour 93%, milk 50%, sugar 10%, butter 10%, egg 10%, instant yeast 1.5%, salt 1.2%. The roux liquid plus main milk totals 85% of flour before egg if both are additional. It is unclear whether the 50% milk includes the roux liquid. Resolve this before mixing; do not silently reduce liquid or present the formula as verified. Baking, portion and pan specifications are also absent. Softness claims are not shelf-life evidence.

## V08 — Brioche format options retained

SRC05’s extended brioche uses the same 50%-butter base concept as S-B06 and adds useful alternative shapes. A 5 kg flour batch has 11,450 g raw dough; at an assumed 2% handling loss, 11,221 g remains. Alternative allocations are 187 × 60 g with 1 g remainder, 140 × 80 g with 21 g remainder, 37 × 300 g with 121 g remainder, or 22 × 500 g with 221 g remainder. These are alternatives, not simultaneous outputs.

For a 500 g loaf, arrange five 100 g balls; for a three-strand braid, use 167 + 167 + 166 g. A brioche à tête may reserve about one fifth for its head. Trial small pieces at 170–180°C for 14–20 minutes, 300 g loaves at 165–175°C for 22–30 minutes, and 500 g loaves at 165–175°C for 30–40 minutes. Confirm crumb set and measured bake loss; the 90–94°C crumb target is a quality check, not a substitute for a validated food-safety process. Raw egg wash is applied only before baking.

Cool plain brioche completely before bagging. A frozen-quality trial at −18°C may compare two- and four-week samples; this does not establish a selling period or validate freezing raw dough. Keep butter, egg wash, refrigeration, rejects and unsold portions in the cost model.

# 12. Costing, production and a staged launch

Revision note: the original product tables remain baseline recipe estimates. For the added preparation-ahead route, chapter 6 provides extra reheating-loss allowances and new O-A01–O-A04 costs; update cold handling, transport, energy and labour before relying on these margins.

The following are transparent planning estimates, not quotations, current local prices or profit promises. Replace every ingredient and packaging input with a dated delivered supplier quote before using the plan to purchase or price stock. Whole milk powder and skim milk powder are different inputs. Edible-weight conversion matters: a 1,000 FCFA/kg gross ingredient with 20% trim loss costs 1,250 FCFA/kg usable, before labour.

Egg example: a 50 g edible egg at the assumed 2,200 FCFA/kg costs 110 FCFA. This is a price assumption, not a local market observation. Quotes for milk sold by litre must be converted with measured density; do not silently treat oil litres as kilograms. Packaging assumptions include the named container, with a food-safe stick for popsicles and a spoon/straw where relevant.

## How the model works

1. Batch ingredient cost = sum of ingredient grams × FCFA/kg ÷ 1,000. For a prepared component, recursively include all its raw ingredients and divide by its usable output, not raw input mass.

2. Ingredient cost per saleable unit = costed batch / stated saleable units. Explicit pastry/dough QC allowances are charged to the product. Full unused filling batches are not automatically charged twice: each assembly card charges only its required usable share, so unused safe stock must be recorded and eventually sold or written off.

3. Variable cost per unit = ingredient cost + packaging + other variable allowance. Contribution = selling price − variable cost. Contribution margin = contribution / selling price. Contribution does not pay the owner until overhead and other obligations are met.

4. Other-variable allowances are placeholders for production energy/diesel, incremental cleaning/water and variable handling/delivery. Provisionally allocate 60% to energy/diesel, 20% to incremental cleaning/water and 20% to variable handling/delivery, then replace with actual logs. Paid shift wages and base route transport are in overhead below; do not charge the same expense in both places.

5. Sensitivity at 20% ingredient inflation: contribution falls by 0.20 × ingredient cost per unit. At 80% sell-through, assume all planned variable cost was incurred, including packaging: effective contribution per sold unit = price − full planned variable cost / 0.80. This conservatively charges unsold units to those sold.

6. All prices and calculations are gross cash planning values without a tax determination. If an applicable tax must be remitted from the displayed selling price, remove it from revenue before computing contribution. Obtain the business-specific treatment locally; no Cameroon tax status is assumed.

## Recipe and energy cost reality checks

Bread uses approximately 278 FCFA ingredients per loaf at the assumed flour price, before packaging and energy. Its 650 FCFA test price is not automatically affordable locally; test against actual competing loaf weights. O-P03 sweet potato bread and O-P20 soaked cake have lower example contribution margins than several snacks. Their roles are differentiation and basket value, not guaranteed volume or margin leadership.

The family loaf’s 55 FCFA other-variable allowance assigns 33 FCFA to energy/diesel provisionally. If an oven load produces 48 loaves, the allocated energy budget is 1,584 FCFA per load. Compare that with measured diesel litres × delivered diesel price plus electricity/battery-related costs. If the actual energy cost per loaf is higher, replace the allowance; do not declare the loaf profitable from ingredient cost alone.

| Example daily fixed/standing cost item | FCFA/day assumption | Measurement needed |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Paid production and sales shifts including owner replacement labour | 18000 | Actual staffing hours, wages and employer costs |
| Rent and standing facility expenses | 3500 | Both premises monthly costs / actual trading days |
| Base transport route | 2500 | Vehicle/fuel/driver allocation; incremental deliveries above excluded |
| Standing refrigeration and backup power costs | 2500 | Metered overnight/standby power and backup cost |
| Equipment and solar/battery depreciation or replacement reserve | 2500 | Asset price, expected life and maintenance plan |
| Routine deep cleaning and pest control | 1000 | Base cleaning labour/consumables not already variable |
| Administration and communications | 1000 | Actual fixed expenses |
| Total illustrative daily overhead | 31000 | Excludes financing, income tax and unexpected losses |

| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| O-P01 Soft family bread | 30 | 650 | 19,500 | 10,902 |

This illustration prepares 283 units in the stated mix, not a demand forecast. Revenue at 100% sale is 108,600 FCFA; planned variable cost 55,844; contribution 52,756. After 31,000 FCFA illustrative overhead, operating surplus is 21,756 FCFA before financing, income tax and exceptional losses. Filling and mix start-up stock can require more cash than these consumed-portion costs.

| Scenario | Revenue | Variable cost incurred | Overhead | Operating result |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 100% sold, base prices | 108,600 | 55,844 | 31,000 | 21,756 |
| 80% sold, all production costs incurred | 86,880 | 55,844 | 31,000 | 36 |
| 60% sold, all production costs incurred | 65,160 | 55,844 | 31,000 | -21,684 |
| 100% sold, ingredient costs +20% | 108,600 | 64,555 | 31,000 | 13,045 |
| 80% sold and ingredient costs +20% | 86,880 | 64,555 | 31,000 | -8,675 |

At full sell-through and unchanged mix, average contribution is 186.4 FCFA/unit; break-even volume is about 167 equivalent units/day, or about 63,814 FCFA sales. If the entire illustrated basket is already produced, its cash break-even sell-through is 80.0%. These are different questions. Changing the product mix, waste rate or staffing changes both thresholds.

## Price ladder and simple combinations

Use visible single-item prices and portion descriptions. Entry: 75 FCFA puff puff, 250 FCFA bun/doughnut, 250–300 FCFA small pop. Everyday: 300 FCFA fish roll, 500 FCFA beef pie, 650 FCFA family bread. Treat: 600 FCFA soft serve or 650 FCFA milk cake. Meal: 1,800–2,400 FCFA pizza, burgers and wraps. These are test points; an expensive meal should not displace the affordable traffic range.

Two milk buns: example bundle price 450 FCFA; ingredient/pack/other variable cost 245.3; contribution 204.7 before overhead. Use only when it adds purchases rather than discounting an order the customer would have made anyway.

Beef pie and small milk pop: example bundle price 750 FCFA; ingredient/pack/other variable cost 406.5; contribution 343.5 before overhead. Use only when it adds purchases rather than discounting an order the customer would have made anyway.

Chicken shawarma and small fruit pop: example bundle price 2,200 FCFA; ingredient/pack/other variable cost 1,195.4; contribution 1,004.6 before overhead. Use only when it adds purchases rather than discounting an order the customer would have made anyway.

For the first week, capture actual supplier cost, produced units, rejects, samples, sales, discounts, unsold units and customer comments daily. Update the CSV inputs and re-run the calculations before changing the menu. The supplied CSVs are editable data tables, not a live automatically recalculating spreadsheet; the accompanying Python builder regenerates the report from changed inputs.

## Two-site production, staffing and energy

## Site responsibilities and transport

Proposed allocation, subject to the actual installed locations: production handles dough, pastry, bread, buns, cakes, cooked fillings and validated frozen-mix preparation. Sales handles shawarma final cooking, burgers, fries, soft-serve dispensing and final assembly. Popsicle production belongs at whichever site has suitable sanitation, rapid hardening and enough power headroom; its unknown rating prevents a firm allocation.

Use separate clean food-safe crates for cooled bread, insulated chilled containers for perishable fillings/mix, and insulated frozen transport for bars. Raw meat travels sealed and segregated below ready-to-eat food. Record dispatch and receipt time, batch, quantity and product temperature. Chilled food must meet H01–H05; hot transport must maintain the house hot standard; frozen products should remain frozen at the operating target. Route duration and container capacity require a measured trial.

The receiver checks seal condition, label, batch identity and temperature before accepting. Reject unknown histories and investigate failed deliveries. Do not put newly hot food alongside chilled dairy, or close hot bread into airtight plastic. Return empty crates through a cleaning route, separate from new food.

## Illustrative daily sequence

| Window | Production site | Sales site | Main constraint |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 05:30–06:00 | Check water, cold logs, battery state, diesel and scales | Receive opening stock/check cabinets when staff arrive | Early electrical work may be battery-fed |
| 06:00–07:00 | Mix and proof first bread/buns; stagger spiral and cooker | Set chilled garnish station; sanitize machine | Do not assume sunrise PV covers mixing |
| 07:00–08:30 | Bake first wave; cooling racks; start pie filling only with cooling space | Sell prior prepared fresh-delivery stock; small fry batches | Separate hot bread from chilled transport |
| 08:30–10:30 | Pie and snack batches; second bread wave if ordered | Morning snack sales; log demand and run safe replenishment | Fryer recovery and hand assembly |
| 10:30–12:00 | Dispatch controlled lunch components; prepare limited frozen stock | Prepare limited shawarma cone/pan portions | No oversized cone based only on oven capacity |
| 12:00–14:00 | Smaller second batch only against sell-through | Lunch service; stagger fryer, hot plate and machine loads | 15 kW sales inverter and compressor surges |
| 14:00–16:00 | Mix/dessert prep during measured solar surplus if available | Frozen dessert and snack peak hypothesis to test | Hardening capacity and battery recharge |
| 16:00–18:30 | Targeted evening bread dispatch; finish early enough to cool | Evening wraps and frozen service | Use forecast, not automatic full batches |
| Close | Waste log, cleaning, cold checks and next-day quantities | Dispose per policy, machine cleaning and safe overnight cold loads | Protect cold storage battery reserve |

## Staffing and work design

Opening working model: one trained lead baker; one prep/finishing assistant with cold-chain responsibility; one sales cook who owns raw-meat controls; one service/cashier person. A relief/delivery role may be needed during peaks and cleaning. These are roles, not proof that four people can cover all hours or all 28 products. Observe labour minutes for the limited menu before adding items. The overhead allowance must be replaced by actual paid hours, including owner work.

Label each weighed kit before mixing. Maintain a batch sheet for dough, filling and finished units. Share O-B07 pastry across three pies/rolls; O-B18 chicken across wraps, pizza and loaded fries; O-B20/O-B28 sauces across defined products; O-B25 buns across burgers; O-FBASE1 across cups, sundaes, shakes and sandwiches. Sharing reduces ingredient variety but never permits unsafe carryover or cross-contamination.

## Demand and replenishment method

Start with customer commitments and small trials. During the first three days, prepare at most one economical equipment-minimum batch per launch product, split between opening and later waves where fermentation allows. Record sales in hourly blocks. Days 4–14: forecast from comparable trading days plus paid orders; prepare about 60% of the forecast first, then release a second batch only when remaining sales and production lead time justify it. These percentages are management trial rules, not demand evidence.

Use a practical reorder trigger: expected sales during production plus delivery lead time, plus a small measured buffer, minus saleable stock already present. Do not replenish perishable stock close to closing simply to make a counter look full. Record unsold stock by cause: forecast error, poor quality, temperature failure, transport damage, portion rejects or customer preference.

## Electrical loads and daily energy

Power in kW is simultaneous demand. Energy in kWh is power × operating hours × duty fraction. Thirty kWh nominal battery energy does not mean 30 kW continuous output. Two separate battery banks cannot share load unless specifically interconnected. The following site allocation, hours and duty fractions are illustrations, not measurements. It assumes one appliance at each stated rating.

## Production illustrative electrical energy

| Load | Rated/assumed kW | Hours on | Duty fraction | kWh/day |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Oven auxiliaries | 3 | 4 | 1 | 12.00 |
| Spiral mixer | 6.5 | 1 | 1 | 6.50 |
| Moulder | 1 | 0.5 | 1 | 0.50 |
| Proofer | 1 | 4 | 0.5 | 2.00 |
| Mixing machine | 2 | 0.5 | 1 | 1.00 |
| Induction cooker | 5 | 2 | 0.6 | 6.00 |
| Slicer unknown assumed | 0.5 | 0.5 | 1 | 0.25 |
| Cold storage aggregate assumed | 1.2 | 24 | 0.5 | 14.40 |
| Lights and small loads assumed | 0.3 | 10 | 1 | 3.00 |
| Total electrical energy |  |  |  | 45.65 |

## Sales illustrative electrical energy

| Load | Rated/assumed kW | Hours on | Duty fraction | kWh/day |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Ice cream machine A | 4 | 8 | 0.45 | 14.40 |
| Ice cream machine B | 1.5 | 8 | 0.45 | 5.40 |
| Shawarma | 3.5 | 4 | 0.7 | 9.80 |
| One induction fryer | 5 | 4 | 0.6 | 12.00 |
| Hot plate/cooker assumed allocation | 5 | 2 | 0.6 | 6.00 |
| Cold storage aggregate assumed | 1 | 24 | 0.5 | 12.00 |
| Lights and small loads assumed | 0.3 | 10 | 1 | 3.00 |
| Total electrical energy |  |  |  | 62.60 |

The popsicle machine, professional pot heating, ventilation, pumps, water heating and any extra fryers/proofers are excluded because their ratings or quantities are unknown. They must be added before this is an operational load budget. Oven diesel is excluded from electrical kWh and must be measured separately. Startup and cleaning cycles can exceed the assumed running duty fractions.

## Simultaneous load examples

| Scenario | Illustrative total | Implication |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Production oven + spiral + proofer + cold + small loads | 3 + 6.5 + 1 + 1.2 + 0.3 = 12.0 kW | Below 20 kW nominal before surges/unknown loads |
| Add production cooker + mixer + moulder + assumed slicer | 12 + 5 + 2 + 1 + 0.5 = 20.5 kW | Exceeds 20 kW; schedule these apart |
| Sales both ice machines + shawarma + fryer + cold + small loads | 4 + 1.5 + 3.5 + 5 + 1 + 0.3 = 15.3 kW | Exceeds 15 kW even before startup surge |
| Add sales hot plate/cooker | 15.3 + 5 = 20.3 kW | Not an acceptable all-on schedule |
| Sales A + shawarma + cold + lights | 4 + 3.5 + 1 + 0.3 = 8.8 kW | One possible low-load service block, still verify phase/surge |
| Sales A + fryer + cold + lights | 4 + 5 + 1 + 0.3 = 10.3 kW | Another block; do not add uncounted hot plate/popsicle load |

Turning a machine off is not a food-safety control. Maintain refrigeration and any active cone’s continuous cooking requirements. If the safe process requires simultaneous loads beyond the inverter’s verified ability, reduce the menu or use a professionally designed supply/load-control change; do not alternate a partially cooked cone into unsafe warm waiting. Check single-phase/three-phase limits and battery BMS discharge capability with an electrician before adopting any schedule.

## Battery runtime illustration

At each site, assume 30 kWh nominal × 80% usable fraction × 90% conversion efficiency = 21.6 kWh delivered AC energy. These 80% and 90% figures are assumptions, not the battery specification. Without solar/grid input, that supports a constant 1 kW load for about 21.6 hours, 2 kW for 10.8 hours, 5 kW for 4.3 hours, or 10 kW for 2.2 hours, subject to discharge power limits. Actual cycling refrigerator loads should be measured over a full day.

A twelve-hour overnight critical load averaging 1 kW needs 12 kWh delivered AC, equivalent to about 16.7 kWh nominal under the same assumptions. Set the evening reserve using measured essential refrigeration plus margin and forecast morning demand. Do not consume the cold-chain reserve on optional fryers or a second frozen-dessert machine.

PV energy cannot be estimated from the inverter size. Use measured site generation or actual installed panel kWp × site-specific equivalent sun hours × system performance ratio. The illustrative daily electrical totals above are about 45.6 kWh at production and 62.6 kWh at sales before omitted loads. Neither is covered merely because a 30 kWh battery exists; daily replenishment and evening reserve matter.

## Launch validation and daily controls

## Phased launch

| Phase | Range and work | Advance only when |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Preparation week | Supplier quotes; oven tray map; five core recipe pilots; probes/scales; machine manuals; transport and power tests | Measured yield, cooking/cooling logs and electrical plan are workable |
| Week 1 | O-P01, O-P02, O-P04, O-P05, O-P06, O-P07; small paid O-P03 taste test | Repeatable portions, positive contribution after waste, staff can replenish safely |
| Week 2 | Add O-P13 plus either O-F01 or O-F05 after its dedicated commissioning | Recorded service drill, cold chain and machine cleaning pass |
| Weeks 3–4 | Add selected O-P09/O-P11/O-P18 and a second frozen format; test standard vs large pop demand | Existing range is stable and added work does not create queues/waste |
| Later development | O-P12, O-P14–O-P17, O-P19–O-P20 and frozen sandwiches/coated bars as justified | Measured incremental demand and margin justify equipment, labour and storage time |

## Pilot approval record

| Test | Record | Proposed acceptance rule |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Formula | Ingredient brand, exact mass, water, dough temperature | No unrecorded substitutions; all ingredients traceable |
| Yield | Raw input, usable component mass, units, cooked mass, rejects | Three successive batches with explained variance; aim within 5% of adopted target |
| Portion | Ten random pieces per batch | Aim within ±3% of target average; no underweight labelled product |
| Sensory | Blind taste from intended customers, not only staff | At least 20 paid/structured test responses per candidate; agree threshold before testing |
| Holding quality | Check at service, 1 hour and 2 hours; longer only under safe control | No significant loss of intended texture at the planned sale window |
| Safety | Cooking, cooling, storage and transport records | All adopted critical limits met; any failure blocks sale |
| Cost | Actual delivered ingredients, oil and packaging | Positive contribution with measured waste; overhead coverage assessed |
| Service | Timed assembly and queue length | Meet practical service target without bypassing hygiene |
| Market | Units produced, sold, samples and repeats | Track 7–14 days before claiming repeat demand |
| Frozen bars | Mould ml, fill g, density, hardening time, seal/stick integrity | No unverified volume claim; stable texture and intact packaging |

The numerical business acceptance targets are proposed management rules, not regulatory standards. Adjust after baseline measurements, but do not lower food-safety limits simply to pass a commercial test. Taste tests do not establish microbial safety. Quantities in this report are recipes for controlled trials until the team demonstrates repeatability.

## Allergens and cleaning

Create a matrix from actual supplier labels. Wheat is present in breads, pastry, doughnuts, wrappers, buns, cake and cookies; milk in enriched doughs, creamy fillings, cheese, many sauces and frozen dairy; egg in egg wash, cakes, egg rolls and many commercial mayonnaise products; fish in O-P06; sesame in O-B35/tahini and optional bun topping; peanuts in O-F02 and O-F08; mustard in O-B28. Margarine, sausage meat, coatings, chocolate and stabilizer blends can introduce soy, milk or other allergens. Supplier labels override this preliminary list.

Use dedicated fish frying equipment or a documented separation plan; shared oil can transfer allergens and flavours to doughnuts and fries. Never label a product allergen-free merely because its formula omits the allergen. Separate raw meat tools, cooked-food tools, bakery utensils and frozen-dessert tools. Clean and sanitize according to the chemical label; do not mix chemicals or invent sanitizer concentrations.

## Power interruption and cold-chain response

1. Record outage start, cold cabinet temperatures and battery state immediately. Keep doors shut and reserve power for food-critical refrigeration.

2. Pause new high-risk preparation if safe cooling or storage cannot be maintained. Use temperature-verified backup storage/transport if available.

3. Record product temperatures and duration, not just the freezer display. Quarantine food with an unknown history; do not assume refreezing restores safety.

4. Restart machines only after safe equipment checks and the manufacturer’s sanitation/recovery procedure. Discard questionable hopper mix and record the loss.

5. Review the event, reserve setting and load schedule before resuming the full menu.

## Daily opening and closing sheet

| Opening | During service | Closing |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Probe/scale check; chilled/frozen logs; potable water; handwash; battery reserve; diesel; clean equipment | Batch IDs; cook/cool checks; hourly stock; portions; machine temperatures; delivery acceptance; actual load peaks | Unsold stock by cause; safe disposal; cash/sales reconciliation; machine strip-clean; freezer log; reserve confirmation; next-day batch plan |

Keep purchase invoices, ingredient lot numbers, preparation dates, temperatures and sales batch links. Avoid statements such as “safe for three days” or “high margin” on staff sheets unless the process and numbers support them. Assign one person to authorize formula changes and update the weighed batch sheet; improvisation should not silently become the standard.

## Additional commercial ideas retained from September

Test breakfast BHB (beignets, haricots and bouillie), school/office packs, pain omelette, sandwich preorders, afternoon brioche and seasonal fruit lines. Beans and porridge were concepts, not complete costed formulas in the gathered recipe cards; develop separate controlled recipes before adding them. Urban menu examples from Douala and Yaoundé show availability, not Nkambe demand. Use customer interviews, paid tastings and actual sell-through.

Use paper/ventilated protection for crisp bread and fully cooled sealed packs for soft sliced bread. Compare slice thickness, loaf weight, bag strength, closure, food-contact documentation, printed label space and supplier minimum orders before ordering packaging. The older 10,000–20,000 printed-bag proposal is a purchasing idea; prove the design and turnover with a small run first.

Track contribution per labour minute and bottleneck hour alongside margin per unit. Separate direct costs, overhead and working stock. Do not treat all produced units as sold. Measure food waste, refunds, cold-chain rejects, energy use and repeat purchases. Set replenishment from real time-of-day sales; do not produce every recipe daily.

The September workbook uses low/base/high stress cases: demand multipliers 0.6/1/1.3; ingredient cost 1.2/1/0.95; energy cost 1.3/1/0.95; energy availability 0.6/0.85/0.98; rejects 12%/5%/2%. These are management scenarios, not observed rates. Keep them separate from the October price illustration.

Its capacity example assumes 16 trays, 5 loaves/tray, 30-minute complete oven cycle, 32 working proof trays, 60-minute proof, 16.513 kg usable mixer dough, 330.26 g raw pieces, 30-minute mixer occupancy, 100 units/hour dividing and 100 cooling/packing. The resulting limiting rate is 100 units/hour before availability; a 6-hour shift at 85% availability gives 510 units of illustrative capacity, not demand. Confirm every input through a time study.

Read the workbook’s Formulas, Yields and Batch plan sheets using the S-series suffixes. The source workbook was inspected, including its formulas and unfilled input cells; it was not recalculated or represented as a completed financial forecast. Use the handbook’s cost method to populate verified quotations and actual operating inputs.

# 13. Quick-reference records and troubleshooting

## Daily batch record

| Field | Entry |
|---|---|
| Date / station / operator / recipe version | ____________________ |
| Batch ID / ingredient lots / actual weights | ____________________ |
| Mixer or treatment load / equipment ID | ____________________ |
| Dough temperature or milk/juice pH and °Brix | ____________________ |
| Proof / cook / treatment readings and times | ____________________ |
| Cooling start, intermediate and final readings | ____________________ |
| Raw, cooked and cooled weights / count / leftovers | ____________________ |
| Packaging lot / allergen label / storage | ____________________ |
| Dispatch and receipt temperatures / quantity | ____________________ |
| Release decision / deviation / responsible person | ____________________ |

## Common defects

| Observation | Check first | Next controlled adjustment |
|---|---|---|
| Dense bread | Flour strength, yeast, dough development, proof | Correct one measured cause; avoid extra yeast without diagnosis. |
| Greasy brioche | Butter condition, dough/proofer temperature | Chill and incorporate progressively; do not hide with flour. |
| Soggy pies or leaking rolls | Filling water, cooling, sealing, pack condensation | Reduce free liquid, weigh filling and verify cooling/ventilation. |
| Greasy fried snacks | Oil recovery, load, piece thickness, proof | Reduce load and confirm temperature before changing formula. |
| Icy or soft frozen bars | Sugar/solids, freezing rate, temperature cycling | Measure Brix and storage history; reformulate only in a new trial. |
| Yogurt whey separation | Milk solids, heat treatment, acidification, shear | Review logs; stabilizer is not a substitute for process control. |
| Juice separation or browning | Fruit lot, oxygen, formulation, package | Trial smaller sealed portions; distinguish quality from safety. |
| Gas, swelling, mould, unusual odour | Spoilage or lost process control | Isolate and withhold; do not taste or reprocess for sale. |

## Working glossary

Baker’s percentage: ingredient mass relative to flour. °Brix: refractive soluble-solids estimate, not a safety test. DDT: desired dough temperature. Overrun: percentage volume increase from incorporated air. Pasteurization: validated pathogen-reduction treatment, not sterilization. Proof: final dough fermentation before baking. Syneresis: whey separation. Usable yield: measured output available for the next step. Contribution: revenue minus the defined variable costs; it is not net profit.

# 14. Sources and edition notes

Source documents are identified by SRC numbers in the separate editorial record. Recipes are inherited trial formulations, not laboratory-tested results. October source links embedded in retained text were supplied with that edition; September references below retain their original access date of 13 September 2026. The preservation references N01–N09 were checked for this edition on 3 October 2026. An inherited citation does not certify every adapted quantity or local market claim.

## Preservation and process references checked for this edition

N01 — [FDA: What You Need to Know About Juice Safety](https://www.fda.gov/food/buy-store-serve-safe-food/what-you-need-know-about-juice-safety). Untreated juice hazards and whole-fruit hygiene.

N02 — [FDA: Juice HACCP Regulator Training](https://www.fda.gov/food/hazard-analysis-critical-control-point-haccp/juice-haccp-regulator-training) and [Juice HACCP questions and answers](https://www.fda.gov/regulatory-information/search-fda-guidance-documents/guidance-industry-questions-and-answers-juice-haccp-regulation). Product-specific pathogen reduction; United States framework used as a technical reference only.

N03 — [National Center for Home Food Preservation: Freezing](https://nchfp.uga.edu/how/freeze/) and [Freezing citrus fruit and juice](https://nchfp.uga.edu/how/freeze/fruits/freezing-citrus-fruits/). Freezing principles and packaging; domestic guidance is not a commercial shelf-life validation.

N04 — [University of Guelph: Pasteurization of Mix](https://books.lib.uoguelph.ca/icecreamtechnologyebook/chapter/pasteurization-of-mix/). Batch ice-cream mix treatment reference, 69°C for 30 minutes; actual equipment/process qualification required.

N05 — [Yogourmet: Original Yogurt Starter](https://yogourmet.com/en/usa/product-details/original-yogurt-starter/). Current supplier instructions; verify the product and sachet supplied locally.

N06 — [Yogurt Made Simple, Washington State University Extension](https://extension.oregonstate.edu/sites/extd8/files/documents/8836/fs173emakingyogurt.pdf). Milk treatment, culture and fermentation principles; home-use holding periods are not transferred to this commercial booklet.

N07 — [Oregon State University Extension: Yogurt](https://extension.oregonstate.edu/newsletter/food-hero-monthly/yogurt-june-2020). Covered refrigeration, mould disposal and texture changes after freezing.

N08 — [UK government: Cooking safely in your business](https://www.gov.uk/food-hygiene-businesses/cooking). Cooking/reheating reference of 75°C for 30 seconds and hot holding at 63°C. These are house operational choices, not asserted Cameroonian statutory limits.

N09 — [FDA: Refrigerator Thermometers—Cold Facts about Food Safety](https://www.fda.gov/food/buy-store-serve-safe-food/refrigerator-thermometers-cold-facts-about-food-safety). Refrigeration, freezing and time outside cold storage.

## S01 — King Arthur Baking

[Brioche: professional formula](https://www.kingarthurbaking.com/pro/formulas/brioche). Publication: undated. Accessed 2026-09-13.

Supports: Develop gluten before progressive butter addition; cold handling.

Limits: Technical basis only; published total must be independently summed. Readable page or indexed primary-source extract reviewed.

## S02 — King Arthur Baking

[Essential baguette tools and techniques](https://www.kingarthurbaking.com/blog/2021/03/26/essential-baguette-tools-and-techniques). Publication: 2021-03-26. Accessed 2026-09-13.

Supports: Gentle shaping, scoring, early steam and subsequent venting.

## Audit of accessible user documents

- Commercial_Baguette_Production_Guide.pdf: reviewed, 2 pages. User-provided draft, not a validation source.
- Staggered_Baguette_Production_Schedule.pdf: reviewed, 2 pages. User-provided draft, not a validation source.
- Cameroon_Bakery_Empire_Master_Guide.pdf: reviewed, 10 pages. User-provided draft, not a validation source.
- Bakery_Empire_Master_Operations_Manual.pdf: reviewed, 4 pages. User-provided draft, not a validation source.
- Bakery_Empire_Master_Operations_Manual (1).pdf: reviewed, 5 pages. User-provided draft, not a validation source.
- Bakery_Empire_Master_Operations_Manual (2).pdf: reviewed, 14 pages. User-provided draft, not a validation source.

## Deliberately excluded or restricted evidence

Bamenda internship upload: not current primary data. Urban menus: availability, not transaction volumes. Santa Lucia indexed yogurt promotion did not appear in the opened current page: no yogurt price adopted. Pizzaland price from the former edition was not reverified: no current pizza price asserted. Old claims of six-month biscuit life, 300% basket growth, guaranteed margins and poor competitor hygiene are unsupported and removed.

## Remaining research gaps

Current Nkambe ingredient quotes, household budgets, paid product demand, competition, utilities, road/route conditions, fruit seasons, exact machine manuals, current full local standards, license scope and all laboratory/factory trial evidence remain unresolved.
